Title: Modeling Discrete Data with High-Order Vector Potts Models

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.03429v1

Published Time: Wed, 03 Jun 2026 01:11:35 GMT

Number of Pages: 89

Markdown Content:
## Modeling Discrete Data with High-Order Vector Potts Models 

A Preprint 

Aaron De Clercq 

Helmholtz Institute Erlangen-Nürnberg for Renewable Energy Forschungszentrum Jülich GmbH, Germany 

a.de.clercq@fz-juelich.de 

Merijn Moody 

Institute for Theoretical Physics Korteweg-de Vries Institute for Mathematics University of Amsterdam, the Netherlands 

m.moody@uva.nl 

Clélia de Mulatier 

Institute for Theoretical Physics Informatics Institute University of Amsterdam, the Netherlands 

c.m.c.demulatier@uva.nl 

June 3, 2026 

Abstract 

Modeling high-dimensional data is challenging, yet essential to understanding many com-plex systems. Maximum entropy models such as Ising and Potts models have been used extensively to capture pairwise interactions from correlation patterns in data, allowing to infer graphical representations of complex systems from observations (e.g., from protein sequences, or neural population activity). Recently, there has been growing interest in modeling higher-order correlation patterns involving simultaneously three or more variables. While progress has been made in binary data with high-order Ising models, we extend this framework to the more general case of discrete data. We introduce q-state spin models, a complete family of maximum entropy models that gen-eralize the vector Potts model to include long-range and arbitrary high-order interactions. Even in the pairwise case, compared to the standard vector Potts model, our models allow for more diverse interaction types. We discuss their statistical interpretation through ex-amples and relate these models to discrete Fourier analysis. Using a loop expansion of the partition function, we show that the statistical properties of q-state spin models are fully captured by the algebraic structure of their interactions. We define gauge transformations under which this structure, and therefore the partition function, remains invariant. Models equivalent under gauge transformations can thus be seen as different representations of the same abstract statistical model, despite potentially having interactions of different orders, extending results from the binary case. For practical application of this framework to data analysis, we focus on a subset of models known, in the binary case, as Minimally Complex Models, generalizing them to discrete data. We obtain a closed-form expression for the marginal likelihood of these models, enabling fast model selection. We illustrate their use with simple real-world examples. 

Keywords statistical inference · discrete data · Potts model · high-order interactions · gauge transformation ·

cluster expansion · information theory · complexity

1 Introduction 

The analysis of high-dimensional data has become a central challenge in modern science, driven by the ex-plosion of high-throughput experiments. Whether analyzing the firing patterns of neural populations, the sequence variations in protein families, or gene regulatory networks, the primary goal is often the same: to infer the microscopic laws or structural constraints governing the system based on macroscopic observa-tions obtained from experimental data. From a statistical modeling standpoint, this problem is frequently addressed using the principle of maximum entropy [1], which enables the construction of the least biased probability distribution consistent with measured observables. Over the last three decades, this approach has been successful at reconstructing graphical models of microscopic interactions in complex systems from discrete data, specifically pairwise Ising and Potts models. More generally, physicists have reformulated the task of extracting information from large, high-dimensional data as an inverse statistical problem [2]. This research has found broad application across the biological sciences, such as the reconstruction of neural func-tional connectivity from population activity [3, 4], the inference of gene regulatory networks from sequencing data [5], and the determination of three-dimensional protein structures from sequence variations [6–11]. Many complex systems however have high-order interactions between their components, which are si-multaneous interactions between three or more components, and can exhibit behaviors that are not well explained by pairwise models. In data, this can appear for instance as high-order correlation patterns between the states of the components (i.e., correlations between three or more variables) that cannot be explained by the pairwise correlation structure of the data. This general problem has led to a recent surge of interest for models with high-order interactions in many fields of complex systems [12, 13]. From a data modeling perspective, finding which high-order interactions are most relevant to explain the data (if any) is particularly challenging due to the combinatorial explosion of the number of possible interactions. Recent progress has been made in addressing this challenge for binary data . To model high-order statistical patterns, References [14, 15] proposed a generalization of Ising-like maximum entropy models to include interactions of arbitrarily high order, which the authors generally call “spin models” (also known in the literature as “high-order Ising models” or “generalized Ising models” [16]). Consider a binary dataset composed of multiple observations of the state s = ( s1, · · · , s n) of n binary variables si taking values in 

{− 1, +1 } (called “spin” variables). A spin model is a parametric family of probability distributions of the form [15]: 

p(s | g, M) = 1

Each interaction is represented by an n-dimensional binary vector μ that indicates which spins are interacting, and the functions ϕμ(s) define how the variables interact, taking the form of product operators:

(2) For example, in a five-spin system, a three-body interaction between s1, s3 and s4 is represented by the binary vector μ = (1 , 0, 1, 1, 0) and corresponds to the operator ϕμ(s) = s1s3s4. Ising-like (pairwise) models only have operators of the form ϕμ(s) = si and ϕμ(s) = sisj between pairs of spins. The vector g = ( gμ)μ∈M is a vector of real parameters, and the normalization factor ZM(g), known in physics as the partition function of the model, is given by:

These values correspond to (high-order) moments of the multivariate systems that are considered as the relevant macroscopic observables by the model. The coefficients gμ are Lagrange multipliers, which are usually fitted so that the model average ⟨ϕμ⟩ = ∑

in M matches its empirical average in the data. The family of spin models is thus able to capture any possible patterns of binary data (i.e., any choice of the multivariate moments). In particular, the complete model with all (2 n − 1) interactions can fit all high-order correlation patterns. In this context, identifying which patterns of the data are relevant is equivalent to finding the subset of interactions that best models the data, which is challenging due to the super-exponential number of possible choices. An interesting property of this family of models, with important implications for modeling, is that it is closed under changes of representation of the spin variables, called gauge transformations by [15]. A gauge transformation is a bijective map of the form σ = b(s), which defines a new basis of spin variables σ = (σ1, · · · , σ n) in terms of the original variables s using a set of independent operators 

b(s) = ( ϕμ1 (s), . . . , ϕ μn (s)) (see example in Fig. 1). The probability distribution (1) of any spin model can be rewritten in the new basis σ by re-expressing each spin operator in terms of the new variables. In the new basis, the operators still have the form of spin operators, and the model probability distribution thus remains that of a spin model 1 [15]. We denote M′ = Tb[M] the spin model resulting from the gauge transfor-mation σ = b(s) of a model M. Crucially, models related by a gauge transformation can have interactions of different orders. In particular, standard pairwise models can map to models containing high-order inter-actions. Moreover, models related by gauge transformations have the same partition function ZM(g) [15], and can thus be understood as different representations of the same abstract statistical model. This gives rise to the concept of equivariant model selection , as illustrated in Figure 1. Suppose we have a binary dataset ˆs written in terms of the variables s (Bob’s dataset) and our model selection procedure yields an optimal model M. If we rewrite the dataset in terms of the new basis variables σ = b(s) using a gauge transformation and perform the exact same model selection procedure to the new dataset ˆσ (Alice’s dataset), it will yield a model M′ that is a priori different from M. One can expect an unbiased model selection procedure among all spin models to yield models that are consistent across different representations of the data. In this example, this would mean that M′ is related to M by the same gauge transformation, 

M′ = Tb[M]. Such a model selection procedure is called equivariant. Because the family of pairwise models is not closed under gauge transformations, equivariant model selection is fundamentally impossible unless we encompass high-order interactions within our model selection framework. This underscores the importance of developing a comprehensive high-order framework for statistical inference. While this framework is well-established for binary variables, a comprehensive framework for discrete data (where variables can take q different values with q ≥ 2) remains under-explored. In this paper, we extend this framework to the more general case of discrete data. Taking inspiration from the binary case, we define a complete family of maximum entropy models able to reproduce arbitrary multivariate moments of the discrete data. This family of models, which we call q-state spin models, generalizes the vector Potts model to include arbitrary high-order interactions, and reduces to the models in (1) when q = 2 . The complete q-state spin models, with all 2qn−1 parameters, can model any patterns of discrete data. More specifically, we can relate this model to a Fourier decomposition of the log-probability. Similarly to the binary case, we identified changes of representation of the discrete system (gauge trans-formations) that transform a q-state spin model into another q-state spin model, with interactions that can be of different orders. Using a loop expansion of the partition function, we show that these models have

Figure 1: Example illustrating equivariant model selection for binary datasets ˆs = {s(i)}Ni=1 , where each datapoint s(i) is a state of a three-spin system s = ( s1, s 2, s 3) ∈ {− 1, +1 }3. A model selection procedure is equivariant if, for a basis transformation of the dataset T : s → σ, the models obtained from the model selection procedure on ˆs (Bob’s data) and ˆσ (Alice’s data) are related by the same transformation T . The left panel shows an example of an equivariant model selection procedure, for which the spin model found in Alice’s data representation corresponds to the model found in Bob’s representation rewritten in Alice’s basis, i.e. MAlice = T [MBob ]. In the right panel, Alice and Bob have the same data as in the left panel, but they can only select among pairwise models. In this case, Bob will find the same model as in the left panel because his model is already pairwise, but Alice’s model cannot be the same because her model has a three body interaction. Instead, Alice will find a pairwise model that likely has similarities with her left-panel model (such as the projection of the three-body interaction onto pairwise interactions), but which cannot be consistent with Bob’s findings anymore. This gives an example where it is clear that pairwise model selection is not equivariant. the same partition function, and thus correspond to different representations of the same abstract statistical model. This defines equivalent classes of q-state spin models, which, in particular, allows for the development of equivariant model selection. We introduce a linear algebra representation of spin models, which provides a comprehensive mathematical framework for working with spin models. In particular, this framework for-malizes the definition of gauge transformations, the characterization of the loop structure and of equivalence classes, and the definition of symmetries in data and models. While the complete model can be used to infer all probability distributions, it is generally over-parameterized for modeling systems with limited data. The challenge of finding a good model for data lies in selecting the most concise description that captures the relevant statistics without overfitting. Ideally, one would compare all possible models to find the one that best describes a dataset ˆs = {s(i)}Ni=1 , i.e., the one achieving an optimal balance between goodness-of-fit and simplicity. Assuming no prior preference between models, we aim to find the model with the largest evidence (or marginal likelihood ) [17]:

. (8) The first term measures goodness-of-fit of the model, while the subsequent terms penalize model complexity, based on the number K of parameters and the geometric complexity cM [15, 22]. Calculating these quan-tities for all possible models is computationally intractable. To remedy this, we generalize the definition of minimally complex models (MCMs), introduced for binary data in Ref. [23], to the discrete case. MCMs are a sub-family of the q-state spin models that correspond to factorizations of the model probability distribution in some spin basis. A remarkable advantage of MCMs is that the model evidence (7) has a closed-form ex-pression, making Bayesian model selection among MCMs feasible. The MCM with the largest evidence then identifies an optimal factorization of the empirical probability distribution that matches the data structure. 7arXiv Template A Preprint 

The paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we define high-order vector Potts models, as maximum entropy models constrained to reproduce high-order moments of a discrete spin system. In Section 3, we obtain a loop expansion of the partition function, which connects the statistical properties of the model to its algebraic structure (the loops of the model), extending results from the binary case to general q. In Section 4, we introduce a linear algebra representation of spin models, which gives a formal framework for working with equivalence classes of q-state spin models. Finally, in Section 5, we focus on the subset of Minimally Complex Models and, in Section 6, we illustrate their use for model selection in real-world discrete data. 

2 Complete family of maximum entropy models for discrete data 

In this section, we define a complete family of parametric models for discrete data. Inspired by the high-order generalization of the Ising model in Eq. (1) [14, 15], we define maximum entropy models able to reproduce any (high-order) moments of a discrete planar spin system, introducing a high-order generalization of the planar Potts model [24] for statistical inference of discrete data. The resulting family of probability distributions extends the maximum entropy models in Eq. (1) to any discrete values of q ≥ 2. In Sec. 2.3, we draw attention to the fact that these models correspond to a spatial discrete Fourier decomposition of the empirical log-probability (or energy function), which has interesting implications regarding the use of these models. Finally in Sec. 2.4, we give some insight into the way these models encode patterns of discrete data. 

2.1 Definitions – q-state spin models with interactions of arbitrary order 

We are interested in studying systems of n random variables that can take q different values (where q is a positive integer). One can map these q values to integers between 0 and (q − 1) without losing in generality. We consider discrete variables in this format and define planar spin variables by mapping these q values to evenly spaced positions on the unit circle. The choice of this initial mapping must be taken into account when interpreting the interactions of the model, which will be discussed in Sec. 2.4. 

Definition 1. Discrete variables: q-state spins and colors. Consider n discrete random variables 

(α1, · · · , α n) taking integer values in [0 , q − 1] :

for all j ∈ { 1, · · · , n }, αj ∈ (Z/q Z) . (9) 

This system can be mapped to the system of n complex variables (s1, · · · , s n) taking values in the set of the 

q-th roots of unity, using: 

for all j ∈ { 1, · · · , n }, sj = exp 

( 2iπ q αj

)

= z αj 

> q

, (10) 

where zq = exp 

( 2πq i

)

is the first q-th root of unity. We refer to the variables sj as q-state spin variables 

and to the variables αj as their colors .

Equation (10) defines a bijection between the two sets of random variables. In the complex plane, the spin variable sj can be seen as a random unit vector whose direction is given by the color variable αj

(see Fig. 2), similarly to the planar spins introduced in the very first version of the Potts model [24]. We denote the state of the system by the vector α = ( α1, · · · , α n) ∈ (Z/q Z)n when expressed in terms of the variables αj and by the complex vector s = ( s1, · · · , s n) when expressed in terms of the variables sj . In the following, we will use interchangeably the notation with dependency in s or in α. For q = 2 , the variables 

sj can take the values +1 (for αj = 0 ) or −1 (for αj = 1 ), just like in the Ising model and its high-order generalization (1). One reason for introducing the spin variables sj in the form of (10) is that they satisfy the property s qj = 1 . In the binary case, this property is useful for defining gauge transformations and to obtain a loop expansion of the partition function [15], both of which we aim to generalize here. Below, this property is also useful for defining a complete set of high-order moments from which a complete family of maximum entropy model can constructed. 

2.1.1 High-order moments, spin operators, and complete models

In the following, we construct a family of maximum entropy models able to reproduce arbitrary-order moments of the spin variables sj , as opposed to moments of the colors αj .

Definition 2. Moments of an n-spin system. For the system of n q -state spin variables sj , there are at most (qn − 1) possible distinct moments, each corresponding to a product of the spins raised to some power between 0 and (q − 1) :

(qn − 1) possible distinct moments. These moments define a complete set of patterns of discrete data, i.e., they can fully describe any possible statistical structure of discrete data. We introduce the n-dimensional vector μ = ( μ1, · · · , μ n) ∈ (Z/q Z)n, such that each value of μ uniquely identifies one of the moments in Eq. (11) (except for μ = 0). The ensemble averages of the spin operators in Eq. (13) define all the moments of the spin system (11) (except for the operator ϕ0(s) = 1 ). Thus, at best fit of the maximum entropy model (12), the values taken by these moments in the model are precisely equal to their values in the data, because these are the patterns the model was constrained to reproduce [1, 25]. One caveat of the model in Eq. (12) is that the term in the exponential can take complex values (as both gμ and ϕμ(s) can be complex), and therefore the state probabilities p(s | g) can be complex. Observe however that for each complex operator ϕμ(s), there is another operator ϕ−μ(s) = [ ϕμ(s)] ∗ that is equal to its complex conjugate 3 in the model (12). These operators correspond to the vectors μ ∈ (Z/q Z)n such that −μ̸ = μ, where −μ denotes 4 the vector −μ = ( q − μ1, · · · , q − μn) ∈ (Z/q Z)n (for −μ = μ, the operators already take real values ϕμ(s) = ±1 ). In other words, the two operators ϕμ and ϕ−μ impose similar constraints to the model. For the state probabilities p(s | g) to be real, we can then impose that:

Number of real parameters. Requiring p(s | g) to be real reduces the effective number of real pa-rameters in the model of Eq. (12). There are two real parameters, aμ and bμ, for each pair of conjugate operators and only one real parameter aμ for each real operator (for which −μ = μ). Therefore, the model has only (qn − 1) real parameters in total (equal to the number of non-zero vector μ ∈ (Z/q Z)n). This is consistent with the number of parameters one would need to model the probability distribution by its 

(qn − 1) independent state probabilities (where the “ −1” comes from normalization). Because the model (12) (resp. (16)) provides an alternative complete description of the discrete system, there exits a bijection be-tween the (qn − 1) state probabilities and the (qn − 1) real parameters of the model. This means that for any distribution of the state probabilities 6 p(s), there is a unique value of the vector of model parameters g

that creates this distribution, which will be given in Eq. (46) (resp. (47)). 

2.1.2 Families of maximum entropy models for discrete data 

Equation (12) with the constraints (15) gives the most general parametric model able to reproduce all possible patterns of discrete data, expressed under the form of the multivariate moments (11). In the context of statistical modeling, besides being able to reproduce the data patterns, one also aims to distinguish patterns that are significant from those that are just noise (for example, in order to extract structural information about the modeled system, or for the model to better generalize). In other words, one aims to reduce the set of parameters of the model to only those that captures relevant patterns of the data. Identifying these relevant parameters can be addressed by comparing (sub-)models of the complete model, in which different sets of parameters have been exactly fixed to zero (before fitting), and selecting the best among them based on model selection criteria, as discussed in the introduction. In this context, the sub-models of (12), for which some of the complex parameters gμ’s have been set exactly to zero, form a complete family of maximum entropy models constrained on reproducing arbitrary subsets of moments of the spin variables from the data. Each of these sub-models is identified by the choice of a subset M = {μ1, · · · , μK } ⊆ [( Z/q Z)n]∗ of operators for which the parameters gμ are not set to zero and which identify the fitted moments. We use this notation to define the family of q-state spin models. 

Definition 5. ( q-state spin models) Complete family of maximum entropy models constrained on reproducing arbitrary sets of spin moments. Consider a system of n q -state spin variables (10) 

s = ( s1, · · · , s n). A q-state spin model is a parametric probability distribution of the form: 



p(s | g, M) = 1

ZM(g) exp 

 ∑

> μ∈M

gμ ϕμ(s)

 , (19) with ∀μ ∈ M , −μ ∈ M and g−μ = g∗ 

> μ

, (20) 

where the set M ⊆ [( Z/q Z)n]∗ identifies the empirical moments ⟨ϕμ(s)⟩ that the model is constrained to reproduce when fitting the parameters gμ’s. The spin operators ϕμ(s) are given by Eq. (13) and can be of arbitrary order. For the probabilities p(s | g, M) to be real, complex operators must be included by conjugate pairs in M and the corresponding parameters must satisfy Eq. (20) . The normalization factor ZM(g) is the partition function of the model: 

ZM(g) = ∑

where g is a vector containing all the parameters gμ’s and the first sum is over the qn states of s. The spin models in Eq. (19) can also be expressed in terms of the discrete variables α, by simply replacing the 

> 6assuming that all the probabilities are strictly positive

11 arXiv Template A Preprint 

dependencies in s by dependencies in α.For binary systems ( q = 2 ), all the operators take real (binary) values ϕμ(s) ∈ {± 1}, the parameters gμ are real, and one recovers the family of high-order Ising models defined in Eq. (1) [14, 15]. 

Note that, if we relax the constraint that operators must be included in the model by conjugate pairs with conjugate parameters satisfying (20), then the energy function in the exponential can take complex values. This implies complex state probabilities and a complex partition function. Although this may not be physical for classical systems, it could still be worthy of study. For instance, extending the partition function to the complex plane has been commonly used to study phase transitions in finite size classical many-body systems [26], including on complex networks [27, 28]. In the spin model (19), upon fitting the complex parameter gμ, the model reproduces both the real and the imaginary parts of the corresponding moment ⟨ϕμ⟩. More generally, one could choose to set to zero any of the (qn − 1) real parameters aμ and bμ in Eq. (16). This is different from the previous setting, because it allows the modeler to choose to reproduce only the real or the imaginary part of a given moment (as opposed to both of them simultaneously). This approach then corresponds to defining the following family of maximum entropy models, constrained to reproduce the real and/or the imaginary part of a selected set of moments of the spin variables: 

Definition 6. Complete family of maximum entropy models for discrete data. Consider a sys-tem of n q -state discrete variables (9) α = ( α1, · · · , α n). The following exponential family of probability distributions defines a complete family of parametric models for discrete data: 

p(α | g, M) = 1

where aμ and bμ are real parameters. M0 identifies an arbitrary set of real moments (for μ = −μ), 

⟨ϕμ(α)⟩ = ⟨φμ(α)⟩, that the model is constrained to reproduce, and MRe and MIm identify two arbi-trary sets of complex moments (for μ̸ = −μ) for which the model is constrained to reproduce respectively the real part ⟨φμ(α)⟩ and the imaginary part ⟨ψμ(α)⟩. These sets are chosen such that: 



where the ordering between vectors μ ∈ (Z/q Z)n is based on the ordering of the integers represented by μ

in base q. The condition that μ < −μ is used so that MRe and MIm only contains one vector μ for each conjugate pair ( ∀μ ∈ M Re , −μ /∈ M Re ). 

Special cases: 

– For odd values of q , M0 = ∅.– The sets MRe and MIm can overlap. When these sets are equal, the model parametric distribution in Eq. (22) is identical to the one in Eq. (19) with M = M0 ∪ M Re ∪ (−M Re ), and the parameters 

aμ and bμ are related to the gμ’s by gμ = ( aμ − ib μ)/2 = g∗−μ for all μ ∈ M Re and gμ = aμ for all 

μ ∈ M 0.– For the binary case ( q = 2 ), MRe = MIm = ∅ and, for all μ ∈ M 0, φμ(α) = ϕμ(α) = ( −1) α·μ.We recover the family of binary spin models defined in Eq. (1) .

The families of models in Eq. (19) and Eq. (22) both generalize the family of binary spin models in Eq. (1) to values of q ≥ 2.

2.1.3 Interactions of arbitrary order between the variables 

In the maximum entropy models (19) and (22), the term in the exponential, which we denote −H(s), can be interpreted as (minus) the energy of the spin system, resulting from interactions between the spins. More precisely, the q-state spin models in Eq. (19) have the same form as the binary models in Eq. (1), 12 arXiv Template A Preprint 

but generalized to q ≥ 2. By analogy to the binary case, one can interpret the discrete vectors μ as encoding interactions (of arbitrary order) between the spins sj for which μj̸ = 0 , with an interaction energy 

Hμ(s) = −gμ ϕμ(s) = −gμ

∏nj=1 sμj 

> j

that depends only on the state of these variables. However, unlike the binary case, the contribution of each spin to the interaction energy is now weighted differently depending on the value of the μj ’s, and therefore there can be different types of interactions involving the same set of spins. For example, for a binary system (1) with n = 4 spins, the vector μ = (1 , 1, 0, 1) identifies the spin operator ϕμ(s) = s1s2s4 and is interpreted as encoding a 3-body interaction between the spins s1, s2, and 

s4. In the ternary case q = 3 , the vector μ = (2 , 1, 0, 1) identifies the spin operator ϕμ(s) = s21s2s4 and can similarly be interpreted as encoding a 3-body interaction between the same spins, but the spin s1 is now weighted twice in the interaction. The vector μ = (1 , 1, 0, 1) also encodes a 3-body interaction between the same spins, but it has a different interaction energy. Indeed, different values of μj weight the colors αj

differently in the operator ϕμ(α) = exp ((2 iπ/q ) ∑nj=1 μj αj

), thus resulting in different contributions to the energy landscape. In Sec. 2.4, we discuss the interpretation of different types of interaction involving the same spins. Similarly, in Eq. (22), each vector μ can be interpreted as encoding an interaction, whose energy Hμ(α) = 

−aμφμ(α) − bμψμ(α) = −aμ cos((2 π/q ) μ · α) − bμ sin((2 π/q ) μ · α) only depends on the values taken by the spins sj for which μj̸ = 0 . More generally: 

Definition 7. Arbitrary-order interactions. For a system of n random variables α = ( α1, · · · , α n),one can identify an interaction of arbitrary order by: – an n-dimensional vector μ ∈ Nn, whose non-zero entries identify which variables interact, – and an interaction energy Hμ(α) that defines how the variables interact and whose functional form depends only on the variables αj for which μj̸ = 0 . For example, for the models discussed in this paper, Hμ(α) depends on the scalar product μ · α = ∑ 

> j

μj αj .

In the cases considered here, the interaction energies, Hμ(α) = gμϕμ(α) or Hμ(α) = aμφμ(α) + bμψμ(α),are non-linear functions of a linear combination μ · α = ∑ 

> j

μj αj of the colors of the spins involved in the interaction encoded by μ.

Definition 8. Order of an interaction. The order of an interaction corresponds to the number of variables involved in the interaction. For an interaction identified by a vector μ, it is given by the numbers of non-zero entries in μ, which is |μ| = ∑nj=1 (1 − δ0,μ j ). Note that this is not the same as the order of the operator identified by μ (or of the corresponding moment), which is ∑ni=1 μi.

2.2 Comparison with other models for discrete systems 2.2.1 Examples of pairwise discrete models Example 1. The planar Potts model [24] (also known as vector Potts model or clock model). In this model, spins take q discrete values uniformly distributed around the unit circle, as in Eq. (10) , and are placed on a lattice or, more generally, on the nodes of a network. Spins connected by a link in the network interact and the Hamiltonian of the system is given by: 

H(α) = − ∑

> ⟨i,j ⟩

Jij cos 

( 2πq (αi − αj )

)

, (24) 

where ⟨i, j ⟩ denotes the pairs of nodes i, j that are connected by a link in the network. In the original model, there is only one parameter, Jij = J for all pairs ⟨i, j ⟩. Here, we adopted a more general notation, where the coupling parameters Jij can take different values. 

The models we introduced in (19) and (22) can be seen as high-order generalizations of the planar Potts model. Indeed, it is straightforward to check that the Hamiltonian in Eq. (24) corresponds to a model of the form of Eq. (22), in which bμ = 0 for all μ and all the interactions are pairwise of the type: for each pair of 13 arXiv Template A Preprint 

nodes (i, j ) connected by a link, there is one interaction vector μ such that 7

μi = 1 , μj = ( q − 1) , and μk = 0 ∀k̸ = i, j . (25) The corresponding operator φμ(α) in (22) is then given by:

This produces a model with antisymmetric interactions in the values taken by the pair αi and αj .Although the planar Potts model wasn’t introduced in the context of statistical inference, one may wonder if it could be used to model the pairwise structure of discrete data. In Eq. (24), each pairwise interaction is parameterized by 1 parameter. If we were to also fit the corresponding imaginary part, we would have 2 parameters. However, to fully parameterize all the pairwise moments based on a given pair of spins, one needs a total of (q − 1) 2 real parameters 8. For example, for q = 3 and n = 2 spins, one needs 4 real parameters: 2 to parameterize the conjugate operators (1 , 2) and (2 , 1) , and 2 for the conjugate operators 

(1 , 1) and (2 , 2) (see Sec. 2.4 for a discussion on the meaning of these different interactions). These latter are not accounted for in Eq. (24), but are needed to fully parameterize statistical patterns of pairs of spins. In the context of statistical inference, a common parametric model that can account for all first and second order patterns of discrete data is the following. 

Example 2. Generalized pairwise Potts model for statistical inference. A generalization of the Potts model commonly used in statistical inference [2] takes the form: 

H(α) = ∑

> <i,j>

Jij (αi, α j ) + ∑

The form of this model is different from the one in Eq. (19) and (22) reduced to all first and second order interactions. Although each of these models is able to fit all the pairwise patterns of discrete data, the model (29) has more parameters than the number of pairwise constraints in the system. In total, there are 

q real parameters for hi(αi) (one for each value of αi) and q2 parameters for each pairwise interactions (one for each value of (αi, α j )), while there are only (q − 1) moments based on a given spin and (q − 1) 2 moments on a given pair of spins. This overparameterization results in the parameters of Eq. (29) being defined up

Example 3. Continuous limit and XY model. In the limit q → ∞ , the planar Potts model (24) becomes the XY model (after adding one-body contributions): 

H(s) = − ∑

> ⟨i,j ⟩

Jij cos ( θi − θj ) − ∑

> j

hj cos ( θj ) , (31) 

where the variable θj ∈ [0 , 2π] corresponds to the angle that the planar spin ⃗s j forms with the x-axis. Before taking the limit q → ∞ , θj is related to the variable αj ∈ (Z/q Z)n by θj = 2πq αj .

Similarly, defining the angle θj = 2πq αj for each value of αj ∈ (Z/q Z)n and taking the limit q → ∞ , the model in Eq. (22) can be seen as a generalization of the XY model to higher-order interactions, where now the vectors μ ∈ Nn. Note that after taking q → ∞ , θj takes real values in [0 , 2π], but αj remains an integer, 

αj ∈ N.

2.2.2 Examples of high-order discrete models Example 4. High-order generalization of the standard Potts model. Ref. [29] introduced a high-order generalization of the standard Potts [30] model of the form: 

H(α | E) = − ∑

> e∈E

Je δe(α) , where δe(α) = 

{

1 if αi = αj for all i, j ∈ e

0 otherwise (32) 

where is E set of hyperedges e between the variables. δe(α) = 1 only if all the spins connected by e have the same value. When all hyperedges are pairwise, one recovers the standard Potts model. 

15 arXiv Template A Preprint 

Although this model wasn’t introduced in the context of statistical inference, one may wonder if it could be used for this purpose. Similarly to our own case, each operator δe(α) defines a (high-order) interaction between the spins connected by the hyperedge e. However, the interaction energy −Jeδe(α) can only take two values (instead of q in our case) and is unable to distinguish states for which the variables αi connected by e take non-equal values. In particular, it doesn’t allow to weigh differently the contributions of different spins to the same interaction. This is an issue for statistical inference, because one aims to model how similar or different the values taken by the random variables are. Besides, each hyperedge e is only associated to one interaction (compared to (q − 1) |e| in our case). This means that there are at most (2 n − 1) different 

δe-functions, which is not sufficient to form a basis of R[( Z/q Z)n] for q > 2, and therefore a full model of the form of Eq. (32) wouldn’t be able to capture all patterns of discrete data for q > 2.For q = 2 , this model could a priori model all possible patterns of binary data, if the set of δe-functions forms a basis of R[( Z/2Z)n] (this remains to be checked) and after adding terms of order one (self-interactions). If this were the case, this basis would differ from the Ising spin basis studied here, decomposing multi-variate information in a different manner. Note that models of the form (32) with only pairwise interactions e = ( ij ),can be mapped to pairwise Ising models using δij (α) = (1 + ϕ(ij )(α)) /2, where (ij ) denotes the vector μ in which μi = μj = 1 and all the other elements are zero. 

Example 5. Other high-order models for discrete systems. Ref. [31] recently introduced another model for the statistical inference of three-state systems inspired by the Blume-Capel model [32, 33] from condensed matter. This model corresponds to a maximum entropy model constrained on reproducing multi-variate moments of the discrete variables α, instead of moments of the spin variables s. So far, this model only includes interactions up to pairwise, but can easily be generalized by constraining the model on higher-order moments as well. Another approach in the context of statistical modeling is the one by Ref. [34], which defines models constrained to reproduce high-order cumulants from multivariate data. 

2.3 Properties of q-state spin models and discrete Fourier analysis 

In this section, we show that the set of qn complex spin operators (13) forms an orthonormal basis of the vector space of complex functions over (Z/q Z)n, and thus that the model (12) corresponds to a particular choice of basis decomposition of the multivariate log-probability, more specifically to a (spatial) Fourier decomposition. Similarly, Eq. (16) corresponds to a decomposition of the log-probability over the (spatial) trigonometric Fourier basis. The properties discussed below extend results known in the binary case [15] to the more general discrete case q ≥ 2. They are direct consequences of the operators being Fourier basis functions. The advantage of mapping Eq. (12) and (16) to Fourier series is that it enables the use of Fourier analysis tools for statistical inference (e.g., efficient algorithms to compute the Fourier coefficients can be used to compute the model parameters [35–38]). 

2.3.1 Spin models as Fourier decompositions of the log-probability Property 1. Group Ω of complex spin operators. The set Ω of all the qn spin monomials (13) forms a finite multiplicative group with identity element ϕ0(s) = 1 . It is the cyclic group of order q generated by the spin variables s1, s 2, · · · , s n:

Ω .

= {ϕμ(s) | μ ∈ (Z/q Z)n} = ⟨s1, s 2, · · · , s n⟩gen . (33) 

where, for all j, the variable sj takes values in the q-th roots of unity (10) .Proof. From Definition 4, it is straightforward that, for all (μ, ν) ∈ [( Z/q Z)n]2,

ϕμ(s) ϕν (s) = ϕμ+ν (s) ∈ Ω , (34) is also a monomial that belongs to Ω, where the sum μ + ν is performed on (Z/q Z)n (i.e. with modulo q). The inverse of an operator is [ϕμ(s)] −1 = ϕ−μ(s) = [ ϕμ(s)] ∗. In the binary case, each operator is its own inverse. Each element of Ω is at most of order q, as: 

[ϕμ(s)] q = exp (2 iπ α · μ) = 1 , ∀ μ ∈ (Z/q Z)n . (35) 16 arXiv Template A Preprint

Property 3. Orthogonality of complex spin operators. We denote by C[( Z/q Z)n] the vector space of functions from (Z/q Z)n to C endowed with the following inner product: for two complex functions f and h in C[( Z/q Z)n],

⟨ f | h ⟩ = 1

Ω = {ϕμ(α) | μ ∈ (Z/q Z)n}, one obtains that they are orthonormal (see App. A.2): 

for all ( ϕμ, ϕ ν ) ∈ Ω2 , ⟨ ϕμ | ϕν ⟩ = δμ,ν . (39) 

Thus, the set Ω of all spin operators form an orthonormal set of vectors of C[( Z/q Z)n].

Besides, there are exactly qn such independent vectors, which is also the dimension of the vector space 

C[( Z/q Z)n]. The set Ω of monomials thus forms a basis of C[( Z/q Z)n]:

Theorem 1. Complex spin model (12) as Fourier decomposition of the log-probability. The set

2.3.2 Inversion formulas for the model parameters 

The complete models in Eq. (12) and (16) correspond respectively to the complex and real Fourier series of 

log P (α), and thus the Fourier coefficients gμ, aμ, and bμ can be obtained by Fourier transform of log P (α).Equations (46) and (47) below generalize to q ≥ 2 the inversion formulas known for binary spin models [14, 15] (for q = 2 , gμ = aμ and bμ = 0 ).

For systems with up to about 25 spins, this approach can be used to perform direct sampling from any chosen high-order spin model, or to compute exactly the coefficient gμ from the empirical probability distribution. For instance, in the binary case, Ref. [39] uses the WalshHadamard transform to perform exact sampling from high-order spin models with n = 20 spins in order to create benchmark data. Ref. [35–38] also use the WalshHadamard transform to compute efficiently the parameters of specific pairwise and high-order Ising models. Using q-state spin models for data corresponds to decomposing the model log-probability over a specific choice of basis functions (the Fourier basis functions). One could choose to use other forms of basis functions, each of them corresponding to a different way of decomposing the information of the multivariate system into basis patterns [40]. 

2.4 Interpretation of q-state spin interactions 

In this section, we give some insight into the way q-state spin models encode patterns of data. We discuss how spin operators shape the energy landscape of models, and what distinguish different interactions based on the same set of variables (e.g., for q = 5 , there are two types of one-body interaction – see Fig. 4). In Eq. (19), the energy of the spin system (Hamiltonian) for a given model M ⊆ (Z/q Z)n with K = |M| 

operators can be represented as a scalar product in CK :

H(α | M , g) = − ∑

> μ∈M

gμ ϕμ(α) = − g∗ · ϕ(α) , (49) where g∗ is a K-dimensional complex vector containing the values of the K parameters g∗ 

> μ

(conjugate of 

gμ), and ϕ(α) is a K-dimensional complex vector containing the values of the K operators ϕμ(α) evaluated on the state α (both vectors are indexed by μ). The energy of the system is minimal if these two vectors are aligned and maximal if they are anti-aligned. However, this is difficult to visualize or interpret in the 

K-dimensional space. Besides, for the Hamiltonian H(α | M , g) to be real, we must take into account that 

g−μ = g∗ 

> μ

for all μ̸ = −μ. In the following, we focus on the contribution of pairs of conjugate operators 

Hμ(α) = −gμ ϕμ(α) − g∗ 

> μ

Let’s discuss on the contribution Hμ(α) of a given interaction to the energy of the system. For a given interaction μ, the direction of the operator ⃗ ϕμ(α) in the complex plane rotates as one changes the state of the variables α (see black arrows in Fig. 3.a and 4.a). The contribution of an interaction μ to the energy of the state α then depends on how well the direction of the operator ⃗ ϕμ(α) aligns with the direction of its parameter ⃗g ∗

> μ

, as expressed by Eq. (51): the better the alignment, the lower the interaction energy, and therefore the higher the (relative) probability P (α) of the state (see Fig. 3.a and 4.a, and interactive widget in Supplementary Material). Moreover, increasing the strength rμ of the parameter amplifies the differences between the state probabilities, making the pattern associated with the interaction more significant and in particular leading to a decrease of the entropy of the operator (see Fig. 3 and 4.b). Overall, in order to minimize its energy, the system will favor states for which the operators ⃗ ϕμ(α) of the model are best aligned with the direction ⃗u ∗ 

> μ

of their respective (conjugate) parameter, while weighting the alignments of the operators based on the relative strengths rμ of their parameters. Below we discuss examples of different types of interactions between the same variables. Note that this effect is specific to non-binary discrete models, as in the binary case there is only one type of spin operator over each subset of spins. u⇤

2.4.2 Different types of interactions between the same variables and examples 

Each interaction vector μ ∈ (Z/q Z)n defines a linear combination μ·α = ∑ 

> i

μiαi of the colors αi of the spins involved in the interaction (for which μi̸ = 0 ). The direction of the spin monomial ⃗ ϕμ(α) in the complex plane results from this linear combination, as ϕμ(α) = zμ·α 

> q

, where zq is the first q-th root of unity. For example in Fig. 4.a, the direction of the operator ϕ1(α1) = z α1 

> 5

rotates by 2π/ 5 each time α1 is incremented 21 arXiv Template A Preprint 

by 1, while the direction of ϕ2(α1) = z 2α1 

> 5

rotates by 4π/ 5. As a result, the operators ϕ1 and ϕ2 order the states differently around the unit circle. Because Hμ(α) increases when the direction of ⃗ ϕμ(α) is further away from ⃗g ∗ 

> μ

(51), this ordering thus constrains the energy landscape Hμ(α) created by the interaction (e.g., states that are directly on each side of the direction of ⃗g ∗ 

> μ

have the lowest energy). In particular, we can observe the impact of this ordering on the shape of the probability distribution associated with a single interaction. For example in Fig. 4.a, the shapes of p1(α1) and p2(α1) reflect the respective ordering of the states of α1 by ϕ1 and ϕ2 around the unit circle. For each model, choosing the states with highest probability (by aligning ⃗g μ with the direction of the operator ⃗ ϕμ(α1) for these states) automatically imposes an order on the probabilities of the other states, given by the ordering of the states around the unit circle. It is not possible for instance to recreate the orange probability distribution in the bottom left of Fig. 4.a with the model p1(α1), no matter the value of the parameters. Indeed in p1(α1),for α1 = 0 to have the largest probability, ⃗g ∗ 

> 1

must be closely aligned with ⃗ ϕ1(0) , which then imposes that 

α1 = ±2 has a smaller probability than α1 = ±1, due to the state ordering created by ϕ1.

Property 8. Effect of different operators on the energy landscape of a model. Different operators yield different orderings of the states around the unit circle, imposing different constraints on the energy landscape Hμ(α) created by an interaction μ. These different orderings are what distinguishes different types of interactions acting on the same sets of variables. Examples of such interactions are given in Fig. 4.a for one-body interactions and in Fig. 5.b for pairwise interactions. 

Another way to think about this is from the point of view of the Fourier decomposition. The differences between the distributions p1 and p2 comes from the change in periodicity of the distributions (frequency is doubled from p1 to p2), which reflects the fact that they correspond to different Fourier modes. We discuss below examples in which certain types of first- or second-order interactions may be more appropriate to model the system. 

Example 6. First-order interactions when modeling nominal or ordinal categorical data. For modeling many systems, it is common to include one-body interactions in the model. However, for q ≥ 5,there are more than one type of first-order interactions, and one may wonder which to include. We saw that each of these interactions encodes an ordering on the probability of the states of the variable αi affected by the interaction (see e.g. ϕ1 and ϕ2 for q = 5 in Fig 4.a). But in general, variables in the studied systems may or may not have a natural ordering. 

For ordinal data, such as answers to questionnaires of the type {“Strongly agree”, “Agree”, “Neutral”, “Disagree”, “Strongly disagree” }, there is a natural order between the values taken by the variables (see example of personality test in Sec. 6.4). In such cases, it is natural to reflect this order, as well as possible opposite valence of the variables, in the choice of the integer value αi assigned to each category. It is then possible that certain single-variable patterns in the data are best modeled by a subset of one-body interactions instead of all (see for instance the patterns in Fig. 4.a). In the example above, a natural choice reflecting the order and opposite valence between the possible answers is to assign values of αi ranging from −2 for “Strongly disagree” to 2 for “Strongly agree”, passing by αi = 0 for “Neutral”. In this case, choosing an informed assignment of the discrete states over other assignments can help with the interpretation of the uncovered interactions. 

For nominal data (e.g., amino-acids in protein sequencing), ordering the categories by mapping them to integer values αi ∈ (Z/q Z)n has no specific meaning and the mapping choice is often arbitrary. In this case, the constraints on single-variable patterns imposed by using a subset of single-spin operators (e.g., ϕ1

or ϕ2 in Fig. 4.a) are a priori arbitrary, and one can expect to include all one-body operators in the model, from which any one-body pattern can be created. In Fig. 5.a for example, the two operators ϕ1 and ϕ2 for 

q = 5 are combined to give the largest probability to a specific value of αi and equal probability to the other states, a pattern that can’t be obtained with a single operator. 

Example 7. Encoding symmetric or antisymmetric pairwise patterns. For q = 3 for instance, pair-wise interactions can be encoded by two different types of spin operators (and their c.c.): ϕ(11) (s) = s1s2 (with c.c. ϕ(22) (s) = s21s22) and ϕ(12) (s) = s1s22 (with c.c. ϕ(21) (s) = s 21 s2). Fig. 5.b shows how these two operators place the states of a two-spin system around the unit circle: states are divided in three groups with three 

22 arXiv Template A Preprint 

states each. Observe that the interaction encoded by the operator ϕ12 and a parameter with direction u∗ 

> 12

= 0 

(along the x-axis) gives the largest probability to symmetric states 13 (α1, α 2) ∈ { (0 , 0) , (1 , 1) , (−1, −1) }, while the interaction encoded by the operator ϕ11 and a parameter direction u∗ 

> 11

= 0 gives the largest probability to anti-symmetric states of the type (α1, α 2) ∈ { (0 , 0) , (−1, +1) , (+1 , −1) } (imagine for instance a system in which αi = 0 is a neutral state, while αi = ±1 encode opposite states). To interpret these observations from a statistical modeling perspective, consider a dataset for two discrete variables α = ( α1, α 2) with q = 3 for which a large fraction of the datapoints are in a symmetric state 

(α1, α 2) ∈ { (0 , 0) , (1 , 1) , (2 , 2) }. For all these symmetric states, the interaction μ = (1 , 2) verifies that 

μ · α = 0 (mod 3) . This means that the operator ϕ12 takes the value ϕ12 (α) = z 05 = 1 for a large fraction of the datapoints, which lowers the entropy of ϕ12 and increases its significance in comparison to the other pairwise operator ϕ11 . Alternatively, if the symmetric pattern is particularly unlikely in the data, this would also reduce the entropy of ϕ12 , thus also increasing the significance of this patterns. Patterns can be significant because they are either more predominant or more rare than what can be expected from chance. Similarly, ϕ11 

would be more relevant than ϕ12 in a dataset dominated by the anti-symmetric state mentioned above. Note that the converse is not necessarily true: a low entropy of ϕ12 doesn’t necessarily imply that the symmetric pattern is dominant in the data, because any of the two other groups of states (orange and blue states in Fig. 5.b) could also be dominant. However, these patterns can be harder to interpret a priori. More generally for larger values of q, the pairwise interaction μ = (1 , q − 1) groups together, in the direction of u∗ 

> μ

= 0 , the states α = ( α1, α 2) that satisfy μ · α = α1 − α2 = 0 , which are the symmetric states α1 = α2 in (Z/q Z). Similarly, the pairwise interaction μ = (1 , 1) groups, in the direction of u∗ 

> μ

= 0 ,the states α = ( α1, α 2) that satisfy μ · α = α1 + α2 = 0 , which are the anti-symmetric states of the form 

α1 = −α2 in (Z/q Z). These types of interactions are thus a good choice to model significant symmetric or anti-symmetric pairwise patterns. In particular, the pairwise interactions used in the planar Potts model [24] (see Example 1) are of the symmetric form with u∗ 

> μ

aligned with the x-axis (as bμ = 0 ); this model thus favors or disfavors symmetric pairwise patterns depending on the sign of the parameter Jij .As an example, the most prominent voting patterns in the US Supreme Court dataset analyzed in Sec. 6.3 are symmetric votes between pairs of justices, which explains why the best basis uncovered in that case (after artificially increasing q) has mostly interactions of the type μ = (1 , q − 1) (and not μ = (1 , 1) as one could naively expect). 

2.4.3 Entropy of spin operators, symmetry and invariance 

A final observation is that each interaction μ divides the space of states into equal-size groups of states that have the same interaction energy Hμ(α). Indeed, by definition (14), an operator ϕμ(α) only takes values in the q roots of unity, corresponding to q locations around the unit circle. The states α for which the dot product μ · α takes the same value correspond to the same location (see example in Fig. 5.b) and thus have the same interaction energy Hμ(α). Besides, there are the same number of states at each these locations 14 , and therefore a single spin operator divides the state space into equal-sized groups of states of equal interaction energy (see Fig. 5.b). A consequence is that a model with a single interaction μ divides the state space into equal-sized groups of states of equal probability, thus encoding certain symmetries in the model probability distribution. If 

q is prime, or if μ verifies for all integer q′ ∈ [0 , q − 1] that q′μ̸ = 0 in (Z/q Z)n, then there are q such groups (otherwise there are fewer groups 15 ). Moreover, each time an independent interaction 16 is added to the model, each group is divided further into q equal-size groups of states of equal probabilities. Here the independence between interactions is defined by the linear independence between the corresponding vectors 

μ in (Z/q Z)n (see Sec. 3.3 on independent models), and at most n independent operators can be added to a

> μ

, from being aligned with one of the q-th roots of unity (i.e., aligned with the operator value of a group of states) to being aligned with another root (i.e., with the operator value of another group of states) results in shuffling the amplitude of the probability distribution over the states, as shown in the first and last columns of Fig. 3.a and 4.a. This effect is reflected in the invariance of the entropy of a model with a single interaction μ when the parameter ⃗g ∗ 

> μ

is rotated by 2π/q , thus giving a periodic structure to the entropy of the model as ⃗g ∗ 

> μ

is continuously rotated at fixed strength (see Fig. 3.b and 4.b). At fixed value of the strength rμ, the entropy is maximal when ⃗g ∗ 

> μ

points exactly between two consecutive directions of ⃗ ϕμ,and minimal when its direction coincides with the direction of one of the vectors ⃗ ϕμ.24 arXiv Template A Preprint 

More generally, the entropy landscape of an operator ϕμ(α) under a spin model with the single inter-action μ as one varies the parameter gμ is the same for all operators at a given value of q (see Fig. A1 for entropy plot at different values of q). This is valid under the condition that q is prime, or that μ verifies for all integer q′ ∈ [0 , q − 1] that q′μ̸ = 0 in (Z/q Z)n. Otherwise, the entropy plot will be that of the smallest value q′ such that q′μ = 0. Indeed, for a q-state spin model with a single interaction μ satisfying the condition above, the energy of the states can only take q different values, Hμ(α) = −2⃗ g∗ 

> μ

3 Loop and cluster expansion of the partition function 

In the binary case, Ref. [15] showed that multiple spin models can have the same partition function (up to permutation of their parameters) and therefore describe the same statistical structure. This was obtained using a cluster expansion of the partition function, expressing it as a sum over the loops of the model: models with the same loop structure have the same partition function. Introducing spin basis transformations, Ref. [15] showed that these models are different representations of the same abstract model seen in different bases. These basis transformations, called gauge transformations in [15], divide the space of models into equivalence classes of models describing the same statistical structure: models of the same class have the same partition function, and thus the same Fisher information matrix and model complexity. They are characterized by the same number of operators, the same rank, and the same loop structure. Here, we extend these results to the more general case of q-state spin models with q ≥ 2, starting with the generalization of the loop expansion of the partition function in this section, and followed by the notion of gauge transformations and equivalent models in Sec. 4. 

3.1 Loop expansion of the (complex) partition function 

For q = 2 , the partition function (3) of the high-order Ising model can be written as a sum over its loops 

L(M) [15]: 

for q = 2 , ZM(g) = 2 n

. (56) In Eq. (56), the sum over μ is taken in (Z/2Z)n (i.e., with a modulo 2) and L(M) contains the empty set 

ℓ = ∅, for which the product over the elements of ℓ in Eq. (55) is equal to 1. Note that Ref. [15] defines a loop as a subset of operators ϕμ(α) of the model whose product is equal to 1 for all states α of the system; this is equivalent to the sum of the corresponding binary representation μ being equal to 0, as written in Eq. (56). Equation (55) implies that all the models with the same loop structure also have the same partition function (up to permutations of the parameters), and thus they have the same statistical properties (in particular, they have the same Fisher Information Matrix). Similarly, for q ≥ 2, we found that the partition function of the high-order discrete spin models in Eq. (19) can also be written as a sum over the “loops” of the model. 25 arXiv Template A Preprint

Equation (57) thus generalizes the loop expansion of the partition function to all q-state spin models for q ≥ 2.The loops of a spin model, defined in Eq. (58), are a set of linear dependencies between the operators of the model that result from the choice of the model architecture (i.e., which operators are chosen in the model). These dependencies capture the statistical properties of the parametric model, as shown by the loop expansion of the partition function in Eq. (57). The properties of the set of loops Lq (M) of a spin model are further discussed in Sec. 4.5. Figure 6 gives an example of the set of loops of a q-state spin model for q = 3 .Note that the figure doesn’t show trivial loops resulting from the structure of complex conjugate operators (see next section). 

3.2 Loop expansion of the real partition function over non-trivial loops (accounting for c.c. operators) 

The expansion of the partition function in Eq. (57) is obtained for any q-state spin model, real or complex valued. In general, operators are not necessarily included by conjugate pairs in the model and the Hamiltonian 

H(α) and the partition function in Eq. (57) can take complex values. In order to interpret a q-state spin model as a probabilistic model, the Hamiltonian of the model must be real. This introduces “trivial” loops that can be removed from the expansion. For the Hamiltonian to be real, operators should be included by conjugate pairs (μ, −μ) in the model and their parameters should satisfy the constraint g−μ = g∗ 

> μ

(see Definition 5). For example, for q = 3 , a model containing the operator ϕμ(s) = s1 must also have its complex conjugate (c.c.) operator 17 ϕ−μ(s) = s 21 . By definition, conjugate operators, μ1 and μ2, verify that μ1 + μ2 = 0 in (Z/q Z)n, and as a consequence they always form a loop in Eq. (58). However, this is a “trivial” loop, in the sense that conjugate operators are included together by construction for the Hamiltonian of the model to be real. This loop thus encodes a “trivial” statistical dependency of the model. A model can have many such trivial loops contributing to its partition function (57). A model with a single pair of conjugate operators has already multiple such contributions. For example, for q = 3 , a

> ϕμ1(s) = s1is identified by the (one-dimensional) vector μ1= (1) , and the operator ϕμ2(s) = s21by the vector

Although these equations encode the same dependence relation, they correspond to two different loops ℓ in Eq. (58) and therefore to two different terms in the expansion of the partition function. Because the constraints imposed by complex-conjugate operators are there by construction, we can compute explicitly their contribution to the partition function and remove them from the set of loops. The latter will thus better capture non-trivial constraints of the model. thus better captures non-trivial dependencies imposed by the model architecture. Note that besides removing c.c. contributions, the approach also removes contributions from combinations of these trivial loops with other loops. 

3.3 Independent models and cluster expansion of the partition function 

In the binary case, Ref. [15, 23] define independent models , as models whose set of operators Mind is an independent set, or equivalently whose set of loops only contains the empty loop, L(Mind ) = {∅} . The cluster expansion of the partition function usually consists of expanding the partition function around the approximation of a “non-interacting” system, which, in the context of spin models, corresponds to the partition function of the model computed as if it was an independent model. For binary systems ( q = 2 ), this is the case of the expressions of the partition function in Eq. (55) and (57), for which having no loop between operators yields the known expression of the partition function of an independent model Mind [15]: 

ZMind (g) = 2 n ∏

> μ∈M ind

cosh( gμ) , for q = 2 . (69) The cluster expansion can then be made explicit by rewriting Eq. (55) under the form: 

for q = 2 , ZM(g) = Zind  

> M

> ℓ∈L (M)\∅

∏

> μ∈ℓ

 , (70) where Zind  

> M

(g) denotes the partition function of M computed as if M were an independent model, given by Eq. (69), and where the sum over the loops now excludes the empty loop. Note that the fact that a model is independent doesn’t mean that the spins don’t interact. An independent model can have interactions of any order between the spins. Instead, independence means that the interactions don’t constrain each other (no loops), so in some sense there is no “interaction” between the operators. Similarly, one can factorize the term corresponding to the empty loop in the expansion of the partition function for q > 2, both in Eq. (57) and Eq. (67). However, this term doesn’t necessarily correspond to the partition function one would get for an independent model. In the following, we give the definition of independent models for complex- and real-valued q-state spin models, which we then use to write the partition function in the form of a cluster expansion. 28 arXiv Template A Preprint 

3.3.1 General expression 

The definition of independent models [15, 23] can be easily extended to q ≥ 2. To do so, we first introduce the notion of independence between spin operators, from which follows a natural definition of independent models. 

Definition 11. Independent set of operators. Consider a system of n q -state spin variables. A set of operators {ϕμ1 , . . . , ϕ μK } is said independent if and only if the set of vectors S = {μ1, . . . , μK } is linearly independent in (Z/q Z)n:

∀(rk) ∈ (Z/q Z)K ,

> K

> k=1

rk μk = 0 (mod q) ⇒ rk = 0 ∀ k . (71) 

This is equivalent to the set of loops of S containing only the null vector: Lq (S) = {0}.

Definition 12. Independent model. A q-state spin model is said independent if the set M of operators of the model is an independent set, or equivalently, if the set of loops of the model only contains the null vector (empty loop) Lq (M) = {0}.

Property 11. Partition function of an independent model: Consider an independent model Mind

3.3.2 Real-valued spin models 

For real-valued spin models, we recall that operators must be included by conjugate pairs in the model for the Hamiltonian to be real. It is clear that two conjugate operators μ1 and μ2 are not independent from each other, as they satisfy μ1 = −μ2. However, we previously obtained a more explicit form of the loop expansion of the partition function (67) by computing analytically the contributions of complex conjugate operators (thus removing them from the loop structure). To obtain a similar form for the cluster expansion, we extend the definition of independent models to real-valued q-state spin models, by requiring independence only for one element for each conjugate pair. 

Definition 13. Real-valued independent model. Using the decomposition of Definition 10, a real-valued spin model M = M0 ∪ M 1 ∪ M ∗ 

> 1

is said independent if M0 ∪ M 1 is an independent set of operators. 

Although the decomposition of M = M0 ∪ M 1 ∪ M ∗ 

> 1

is not unique, this definition doesn’t depend on the precise assignment of the conjugate operators to M1 and M∗

> 1

, because the elements of M1 and M∗

> 1

are related by just a sign change. The absence of dependencies among the operators in M0 ∪ M 1 of an independent model means that the model doesn’t contain any (non-empty) loop, except for the trivial loops 29 arXiv Template A Preprint 

due to c.c. operators in M1 ∪ M ∗

> 1

. Reciprocally, a real-valued model that doesn’t contain any loop, except due to c.c. operators, verifies the definition of independence. 

Corollary 1. A real-valued independent model is a model whose set of loops in Eq. (58) only contains: 1) the empty loop, and 2) trivial loops due to c.c. operators. Equivalently, it is a model whose reduced set of loops in Eq. (68) solely contains the empty loop, Lcc q = {(0, 0)}.

The partition function expansion in Eq. (67) thus already separates the contributions obtained as if the real-valued model were independent, for which (ℓ0, ℓ1) = ( 0, 0), from the contributions resulting from non-trivial loop constraints between the operators, for which (ℓ0, ℓ1)̸ = ( 0, 0).Note that we kept M0 in definition 13, because it allows the definition to be also valid for spin models with q = 2 (which are already real-valued without having to introduce the structure of c.c. operators). Indeed in the binary case, M1 = ∅ and the definition and corollary above are consistent with the previous definition of independent models [15, 23]. For q > 2, any operator μ ∈ M 0 verifies that 2μ = 0 (by definition of 

M0) and therefore doesn’t satisfy the condition of independence in Eq. (71). This means that, for q > 2,independent models have M0 = ∅. The term corresponding to the empty loop ( ℓ1 = 0) in the partition function (67) then gives the partition function of a real-valued independent model for q > 2.

3.3.3 Additional considerations when q is not prime 

When q is not prime, some operators μ other than those in M0 can create “ self-loops ” of the form η μ = 0 ,where η is an integer strictly smaller than q. Just like for the operators in M0, these operators cannot contribute to an independent model, because they don’t satisfy Eq. (71). We call them self-dependent 

operators. Because of these operators, the notion of independent models may seem ambiguous. For example, consider a model for q = 6 with a single pair of c.c. operators M = {μ, −μ} that verifies the self-dependence relation 3μ = 0 (e.g., the one-body operator μ = (2) for a system with a single spin variable). This model has two loops in Eq. (68), the empty loop ℓ1 = (0) and the self-loop ℓ1 = (3) , which yields the partition function (67): 

ZM(g) = 6 n [B0(gμ) + B3(gμ)] . (77) This model is not independent (because of the self-loop). However, from a modeling perspective, a model with a single pair of c.c. operator could be seen as “independent”, in the sense that: 1) it models (at least partially) one dimension of the system, and 2) it doesn’t encode constraints between different operators. For q non-prime, we extend the notion of independent models to also include models with self-dependent operators that do not create non-trivial constraints (loops) with the other operators of the model. We call these models weakly independent 19 .

Corollary 2. A real-valued weakly independent model (for q non-prime) is a model whose set of loops in Eq. (58) solely contains the following loops or their combinations: 1) the empty loop; 2) trivial loops due to c.c. operators; 3) trivial loops due to self-dependent operators (i.e., of the type η μ = 0 with η < q ). 

For models with self-dependent operators, one can get a more explicit form of the partition function by computing the contribution of the self-loops to the partition function (similarly to what was done in Eq. (67) for the operators of M0). The terms corresponding to the non-trivial loops would then capture the correction to the partition function due to the non-trivial dependencies between the different operators (i.e., not due to self-loops or c.c. operators). The contributions of self-loops of the form 2μ = 0, which come from the operators of M0 (when q is even), have been already computed explicitly in Eq. (67). This was done by grouping odd and even powers of ϕμ in the Euler formula (65) for egμϕμ(α). Similarly, consider a self-dependent operator μ ∈ M 1 ∪ M ∗

> 1

and the smallest integer η such that ημ = 0 forms a self-loop ( η is a factor of q). The contribution of this self-loop can be explicitly computed by grouping together the terms that correspond to ϕμ raised to the same power modulo η in the generalized Euler formula of Eq. (63) (using the fact that ημ = 0 implies

3.4 Rank and dimension of a spin model 

The definition 11 of an independent set of operators allows us to extend the definition of the rank of a spin model, originally introduced in the binary case [15, 23], to q ≥ 2:

Definition 14. Rank of a spin model. The rank of a spin model M, denoted by rank( M), is the cardinality of the largest linearly independent set of operators in M. By definition, in an n-spin system, 

rank( M) ≤ n.

For example, by definition 12, the rank of an independent model is equal to the number of operators in the model. For a real-valued independent spin model with q > 2, M = M1 ∪ M ∗

> 1

, the rank of the model is equal to the number of operators in M1.When q is not prime, the rank of the model is not sufficient to capture the number of dimensions in which the model is embedded. Consider for example a model for q = 6 with a single pair of c.c. operators 

M = {μ, −μ} verifying the self-dependence relation 3μ = 0 . The rank of this model is zero, but the model is embedded in at least one dimension. This is because the operator μ lives in one dimension, but doesn’t span the whole (Z/6Z). To clarify this distinction when q is not prime, we introduce the definition of the (embedding) dimension of a q-state spin model. This definition is inspired by the definition of basis of a spin model, which was introduced in the binary case [23] as a minimal set of independent operators that can generate all the operators of the model. 

Definition 15. Dimension of a spin model. The dimension of a spin model M, denoted dim( M), is the cardinality of the minimal independent set of operators that can generate all the operators of the model M.By definition, in an n-spin system, dim( M) ≤ n.

For example, for q = 6 , for the one-spin model M = {μ, −μ} with the one-body operator μ = (2) and its c.c. −μ = (4) , the set with the single operator {μ1 = (1) } is a minimal independent set that can generate all the operators of M in Z/6Z (the operator μ1 is not self-dependent, as oppose to the operators (2) and 

(4) ). The dimension of M is therefore dim( M) = 1 .For models without self-dependent operators, any maximal independent set of operators in the model can generate all the operators of the model, which means that the dimension of the model is equal to its rank. This is always the case for models with q = 2 , and in general for any q prime, for which there is therefore no need to introduce a distinction between rank and dimension [23]. This is also the case for independent models (complex- or real-valued) at any value of q (prime or not). For models with self-dependent operators (which exist only when q is not prime), the dimension of the model can be larger than its rank. In the example above, M = {(2) , (4) } for q = 6 , the rank of the model is rank( M) = 0 , but its dimension is 

dim( M) = 1 .

3.5 Discussion: Loops as algebraic constraints defining the statistical properties of the spin model 

The loops of a q-state spin model, defined in Eq. (58), are linear dependencies between the operators of the model that result from the choice of the model architecture (i.e., which operators are in the model). Equation (57) connects these structural dependencies, captured by the loop structure on the r.h.s., to the statistical properties of the model, captured by the partition function on the l.h.s. More specifically, the Hessian of the log-partition function corresponds to the Fisher information matrix, a covariance matrix that characterizes how susceptible the model probability distribution is to perturbations around any given value of the parameters. The structural dependencies encoded by the loops Lq (M) thus ultimately shape the Fisher information matrix, making the model more or less susceptible to changes in the parameters, or, from a modeling perspective, more or less malleable to fit a wide variety of data patterns. This flexibility of a statistical model can be summarized in a single characteristic called the geometric complexity of the model [21, 22], corresponding to the term cM in Eq. (8) (when the integral is defined). In the binary case, Ref. [15] explores the impact of the loop structure of a spin model (56) on the geometric complexity. 33 arXiv Template A Preprint 

In summary, equation (57) draws a useful connection between the statistical properties of a spin model and algebraic properties of its operators, i.e., the linear dependencies between the operators encoded in the loop structure (58). Equation (57) describes how these structural dependencies constrain the statistical properties of the model. In the following section, we introduce an algebraic representation of spin models, which will allow for a better characterization of this connection. 

4 Gauge transformations (GTs) and equivalence classes of q-state spin models 

In the binary case, Ref. [15] observed that all the models that have the same loop structure (56) have the same partition function (55) up to permutation of their parameters. They introduce bijective transformations of the spin variables (and thus of the state space) that preserve the structure of spin operators, which they call gauge transformations. These transformations map a spin model onto another spin model (with different operators), while keeping the loop structure invariant, therefore preserving the partition function. The transformed spin models can be seen as different representations of the same abstract model. Let us generalize these ideas to q-state spin models. 

4.1 Gauge transformations as changes of spin representation 

Following Ref. [15], we use the notion of independence between spin operators (see Definition 11) to generalize the definition of gauge transformations to systems of q-state spin variables with q > 2. Ref. [23] also introduced a linear algebra formalism for spin models and gauge transformations, which we generalize to larger values of q. Let us start by recalling the definition of independence between spin operators: 

Definition 16. Independent set of operators. Consider a system of n q -state spin variables. A set of operators {ϕμ1 , . . . , ϕ μK } is independent if and only if the set of vectors S = {μ1, . . . , μK } is linearly independent in (Z/q Z)n:

∀(rk) ∈ (Z/q Z)K ,

> K

∑

> k=1

rk μk = 0 (mod q) ⇒ rk = 0 ∀ k . (85) 

This is equivalent to the set of loops of S containing only the null vector: Lq (S) = {0}.

We recall that spin operators are defined in Eq. (13) as products of the spin variables identified by the vector 

μ. Equation (85) thus means that no operator ϕμ in the set can be obtained as a product of other operators in the set raised to any power. Moreover, in a system of n spin variables, an independent set of operators can have at most n operators, because there are at most n independent vectors in (Z/q Z)n.Note that (Z/q Z)n is not a vector space when q is not prime. However, the definition above in terms of linear independence between elements of (Z/q Z)n still holds in this case, as well as the other definitions and the linear algebra representation discussed in this section (see the notion of modules in mathematics [42]). 

Definition 17. Gauge transformations (GTs). Consider a system of n q -state spin variables s =(s1, . . . , s n). A gauge transformation T is a change of spin basis identified by an ordered set of n independent operators (ϕμ1 , . . . , ϕ μn ), where the new basis variables s′ = ( s′

Hence, GTs written for the variables α correspond to linear basis transformations in (Z/q Z)n, which can be identified as the automorphisms of (Z/q Z)n.

Property 18. Matrix representation of gauge transformations. For convenience, the gauge trans-formation in Eq. (87) can be written as the vector-matrix product over (Z/q Z) [23]: 

α′ = α T , (89)

> n

When performing the matrix product over (Z/q Z) in Eq. (89), the elements of the resulting vector are taken modulo q. The n columns of T being linearly independent in (Z/q Z)n means that T is invertible in (Z/q Z)n.Multiplying Eq. (89) on both side by the inverse matrix T −1 allows one to recover the original variables from the transformed ones, thus defining the inverse gauge transformation (also confirming that GTs are indeed bijective). 

Property 19. Inverse gauge transformation. Gauge transformations (GTs) are bijective. Following a GT, the original variables α can be recovered from the transformed variables α′ using: 

α = α′ T −1 , (90) 

where T −1 is the inverse of the matrix representation T of the GT. 

In the following, we will often denote by b = ( ϕμ1 , . . . , ϕ μn ) the set of n independent operators used to define the new basis, by Tb the corresponding gauge transformation, and by T b its matrix representation. The change of basis will then be denoted by s′ = b(s) or, equivalently, by α′ = α T b. Similarly, we will denote by T −1
