(i + 1) -th operator μi+1 such that it is independent from the i operators already chosen. This is because the set of i independent vectors μ already chosen can generate qi different vectors (and thus, different operators) through linear combinations in (Z/q Z)n, and μi+1 cannot be chosen among them. If q is not prime, then for some operators μ ∈ (Z/q Z)n there exists a strictly positive integer c < q 

such that c μ = 0 (mod q) (for example, for q = 4 and n = 2 , the vector μ = (2 , 2) verifies that 2μ = 0 ). These operators don’t satisfy the condition of linear independence, and thus cannot be used to construct a set of independent operators. The total number of GTs in this case is therefore smaller than the number in Eq. (92). Computing the precise number (93) uses notions from group theory and is provided in App. C.1. For example, for n = 1 spin with q = 4 , there are three possible operators, corresponding to the vectors 

μ ∈ { (1) , (2) , (3) }, as μ = (2) cannot be used to define a new basis because 2 μ = 0 (mod 4) . We thus find that the number of GTs in this simple case is |G 4(1) | = 2 , which is also obtained from Eq. (93). Gauge transformations of a spin system correspond to changes of representation of the spin variables. As a result, a GT induces a transformation of any dataset or spin model expressed in these variables. In the following, we discuss how these transformations affect data and spin models. 

4.2 Gauge transformations of data 

Consider a dataset composed of multiple observations (datapoints) of the states of a spin system. For instance, this can be a discrete dataset, composed of observations of the discrete color variables α ∈ (Z/q Z)n. A GT changes the basis in which the spin system is represented and thus induces a transformation of the dataset, which is then observed in a new basis of discrete variables α′ ∈ (Z/q Z)n. A dataset is therefore transformed under a GT by re-writing each datapoint in this new basis. This operation can be performed efficiently using the vector-matrix product in Eq. (89), where T is the matrix representing the GT, and α and α′ are the discrete vectors representing respectively the original and the new value of a given datapoint. Defining the data matrices D and D′, whose i-th row contains the i-th datapoint written respectively in the original and in the new basis, the GT of a dataset can be obtained by the matrix product over (Z/q Z):

D′ = D T . (94) Because a gauge transformation corresponds to a change of basis, there is no loss of information between the original and the transformed data; the new dataset is just the old one represented in a different basis. Under a gauge transformation, each state α ∈ (Z/q Z)n of the discrete system is mapped to a unique new state α′ ∈ (Z/q Z)n. A gauge transformation thus draws a bijection in state space, between the qn states of the original variables and the qn states of the new variables. As a result, the empirical distribution (i.e., frequency of occurrence of the states) remains unchanged, but the states corresponding to each frequency are now shuffled. 

4.3 Gauge transformations of q-state spin models 

GTs are changes of the basis in which the spin variables are represented. As a result, spin operators, which are functions of the spin variables, are also modified under GTs. Remarkably, by construction of GTs, the 36 arXiv Template A Preprint 

transformation of a spin operator remains a spin operator, and as a result, a spin model is transformed into another spin model, as described in this section. 

Definition 18. Transformed operator. A spin operator ϕμ(s) is transformed under a GT Tb by rewriting

Tb[ϕμ]( s′) .

As the transformation of a spin operator under a GT is also a spin operator, the new Hamiltonian remains that of a spin model, and takes the following form. 

Definition 19. Transformed model. Under a gauge transformation Tb, the Hamiltonian of a spin model, with operators M and parameters g, is rewritten in the new basis s′ = b(s). This gives the Hamiltonian of a new spin model M′ with parameters g′ (see App. C.2):

The permutation is such that the transformed operator ϕμ′

= Tb[ϕμ] in M′ is parameterized by the same parameter as the operator ϕμ in M.

Because GTs are bijections of the state space, the gauge transformation of a spin model maps the energy of a state s to another (unique) state s′, thus drawing a one-to-one mapping in state space that rearranges the energy landscape. In the context of real-valued q-state spin models, this yields a permutation of the state probabilities in the model probability distribution, just like the one described for GTs of data. Figure 6.a gives an example of a spin model for q = 3 and of a gauge transformation of that model. Observe that the order of the operators is not preserved under GT: the top model has only operators up to order 2 (i.e., operators connecting at most two variables), whereas the transformed model at the bottom has an operator of order 3 (and its c.c.), connecting all three variables. In general, the order of an operator is not preserved under GT. In Sec. 4.6, we will discuss properties of spin models that remain invariant under GT. For convenience, the gauge transformation of a spin model can be written as a matrix product. To do so we first introduce a matrix representation of spin models.

The definitions and notations introduced so far for GTs are valid for all q-state spin models (with real or complex Hamiltonian). For q-state spin models to be probabilistic models, their Hamiltonian must be real, which requires operators to be included by conjugate pairs in the model (see Definition 5). In the following, we discuss further simplifications in this case.

Figure 6: a) Example of a gauge transformation between spin models for q = 3 . Graphical rep-resentation of the 3-spin model ˜M = {s1, s 21, s 2, s 22, s 1s2, s 21s22, s 1s23, s 21s3, s 2s3, s 22s23} (top) and of the model 

˜M′ = {σ2σ3, σ 22 σ23 , σ 1σ22 σ23 , σ 21 σ2σ3, σ 1, σ 21 , σ 2, σ 22 , σ 1σ22 , σ 21 σ2} (bottom) obtained from the gauge transforma-tion T of M. Because these models consist of 5 pairs of conjugate operators, we only represented one operator for each pair, choosing arbitrarily ˜M1 = {s1, s 2, s 1s2, s 1s23, s 2s3} for ˜M and ˜M′

1 = T [ ˜M1] = 

{σ2σ3, σ 1σ22 σ23 , σ 1, σ 2, σ 1σ22 } for ˜M′. Variables are indicated by circles labeled from 1 to 3. One-body spin operators are represented by colored circles on a single variable, pairwise interactions by lines between two variables, and 3-body interactions by triangles connecting three variables. The colors indicate the power to which a variable is raised within an interaction: blue for 1 and red for 2. For example, the operator s1s23

is indicated by a bicolor line, blue on the side of s1 and red on the side of s3. Observe that the top model is pairwise (i.e., it has only operators up to second order), while the bottom model obtained after GT is not. The matrix forms of the two models and of the GT are given in Eq. (103) and (104). b) Example of the loop structure of a model for q = 3 . Graphical representation of the loops Lcc q of the spin model 

M in panel a) Top, excluding trivial loops due to c.c. operators (which are of the type μ1 + μ2 = 0 , and their combinations with other loops). Operators are visualized using the same convention as in panel a). In this example, the model can be expressed as M = M1 ∪ M ∗

All the loops of the model in Lcc q (M) can be obtained from two generators (see Sec. 4.5). Here, we chose the two loops on the top left as generators, represented by the vectors denoted ℓa and ℓb; the other loops are obtained by linear combinations of these two vectors. The two-dimensional coordinate vector below each loop diagram indicates which combination of ℓa and ℓb

produced the loop. For instance, the last loop of the first row corresponds to ℓa + 2 ℓb. We organized the diagrams such that each loop in the second row corresponds to the complex conjugate of the loop directly above it (notice that their coordinate vectors are conjugate for q = 3 ). of the hypergraph representing the model: each column of the matrix encodes the weighted (hyper)-edge corresponding to one of the operator μ of the model, with weights given by the μj ’s. By default, operators of a real-valued spin model are either their own conjugate or included by conjugate pairs (see Definition 5), as in the examples above. We can thus simplify the representation of a spin model by keeping only one operator for each conjugate pair, both in the graphical representation of the model (as in Fig. 6.a) and in its matrix representation. Definition 21. Reduced representation of real-valued spin models. Consider a real-valued q-state spin model M. Using the decomposition of Definition 10, M = M0 ∪ M 1 ∪ M ∗

Besides simplifying the representation of real-valued spin models, the reduced matrix representation can also be used for the gauge transformation of these models, thanks to the following property (see proof in App. C.3): 

Property 23. Conjugate operators (μ, −μ) stay conjugate under a GT: T [−μ] = −T [μ].

Therefore, for each conjugate pair (μ, −μ), the GT of the operator non-represented in the reduced model, let’s say −μ, is the conjugate of the GT of the represented operator μ. Manipulations of the model such as gauge transformations can thus be directly performed on the reduced model, and the GT of the full model 

M′ = T [M] can be recovered from the GT of the reduced model M′

4.5 Algebraic representation of q-state spin models 

As hinted at by the matrix representations introduced above, spin models can be studied from a linear algebra perspective. In this section, we formalize this framework by introducing a linear map representation of spin models. 

Definition 22. Linear map representation of spin models. Consider a system of n discrete variables 

(α1, . . . , α n) ∈ (Z/q Z)n and a q-state spin model M for this system, specified by the choice of K operators, 

M = {μ1, . . . , μK } ⊆ [( Z/q Z)n]∗. The model M can be represented as a linear map fM from (Z/q Z)K to 

(Z/q Z)n which defines each operator of the model in terms of the state variables. We denote (u1, . . . , uK )

a basis of the domain U = ( Z/q Z)K , which can be thought of as the model’s space; we associate each of these basis elements with one of the K operators of the model M. We denote (v1, . . . , vn) a basis of the codomain V = ( Z/q Z)n, which can be thought of as the space of the spin system, and associate each of these basis elements with one of the n discrete (color) variables of the system. The linear map fM is defined by specifying the following image for each basis element of U:for all k ∈ { 1, . . . , K } , fM(uk) = 

> n

∑

> j=1

μ(j) 

> k

vj = μk , (106) 

where the coefficient μ(j) 

> k

corresponds to the j-th element of the vector μk ∈ (Z/q Z)n defining the k-th operator of M. Here the vector μk = ( μ(1)  

> k

, . . . , μ (n) 

> k

) is considered as an element of the codomain. 

40 arXiv Template A Preprint 

Following this approach, the matrix representation of the linear map fM defined in Eq. (106) corresponds precisely to the n×K-matrix M introduced in Definition 20 as the matrix representation of the spin model M.Note that (Z/q Z) is a vector space when q is prime, it is a module otherwise. The linear map representation is valid in both cases. For example, the model M shown in Fig. 6.a top, with K = 10 operators over n = 3 

spins, corresponds to the following linear map: fM : ( Z/3Z)10 → (Z/3Z)3,



fM(u1) = μ1 = v1 ; fM(u2) = μ2 = 2 v1

fM(u3) = μ3 = v2 ; fM(u4) = μ4 = 2 v2

fM(u5) = μ5 = v1 + v2 ; fM(u6) = μ6 = 2 v1 + 2 v2

fM(u7) = μ7 = v1 + 2 v3 ; fM(u8) = μ8 = 2 v1 + v3

fM(u9) = μ9 = v2 + v3 ; fM(u10 ) = μ10 = 2 v2 + 2 v3

, (107) whose matrix representation is the same as the matrix representation M of the spin model given in Eq. (103) (we labeled the operators in the same way in Eq. (107) and (103)). Figure 7 gives an illustration of the linear map representation of q-state spin models. The state of the system is characterized by the state of the n discrete random variables αj (colors of the spins) and is represented in the system space V by the random vector α = ∑nj=1 αj vj , corresponding to the coordinate vector α = ( α1, · · · , α n). The state of the model is characterized by the state of the K operators of the model. We denote by mk ∈ (Z/q Z) the color of the k-th spin operator (the state of this operator is thus zmk 

> q

). With this notation, the state of the model is then described by the K discrete random variables mk and is represented in the model space U by the random vector m = ∑Kk=1 mkuk = ( m1, · · · , m K ). Using the linear map fM, each operator uk ∈ U of the model M is given a representation μk ∈ V in the system space. The state mk of the k-th operator when the system is in a state α is then given by the dot product mk = α · μk

in V (because ϕμk (α) = zα·μk 

> q

= zmk 

> q

). We can thus obtain the state of the model m ∈ U when the system is in the state α ∈ V by the vector-matrix product m = α M over (Z/q Z), where α is considered as a row vector and M is the matrix representation of fM.In an n-spin system, all the statistical properties of the system are captured by the ensemble averages of each the (qn − 1) spin operators (i.e., all the high-order moments). In this framework, each spin operator whose statistics is constrained by the model has two representations: one in the system space V and one in the model space U. In the system space , any spin operator can be written as a product of spin variables (monomials). This product corresponds to a linear combination of the color variables αi, which is represented by a vector μ ∈ V . The random variable α · μ ∈ (Z/q Z), obtained from the dot product of μ

with the random state vector α, then characterizes the state of the operator ϕμ(α) = zα·μ 

> q

when the system is in the state α. In the model space , any spin operator that can be written as a product of operators of the model (monomials over the operators) is represented as a vector u = ∑ 

> k

ukuk = ( u1, . . . , u K ) ∈ U . For example, the vector u = u1 + 2 u2 ∈ U represents the spin operator ϕu(m) = ϕu1 (m)[ ϕu2 (m)] 2, where the operators ϕuk (m) are the operators of the model. The random variable m · u ∈ (Z/q Z), obtained from the dot product of u with the random state vector m, then characterizes the state of the operator ϕu(m) = zu·m

> q

when the model is in the state m. For a chosen model M, the linear map fM associates each operator represented by u = ∑Kk=1 ukuk ∈ U in model space to a representation μ = fM(u) = ∑Kk=1 ukμk ∈ V 

in the system space. The state of the spin operator can then be equivalently written in model space as 

m · u = ∑ 

> k

mkuk and in system space as α · μ = ∑ 

> k

ukα · μk = ∑ 

> k

ukmk (i.e., ϕu(m) = ϕfM(u)(α)). If the rank of the model is smaller than n, then not all spin operators in V have a representation in the model space U. The operators that can be decomposed in U are those whose statistical properties are controlled by the statistics of the operators of the model (namely the relevant statistics). The other operators are given a uniform distribution in the model 22 . Different operators u ∈ U can be mapped by fM to the same operator μ ∈ V , which means that the same spin operator (expressed in terms of the system’s variables α)can be decomposed in different ways over the operators of the model 23 . Finally, the operators u ∈ U that

are mapped to 0 ∈ V correspond to the loops of the model (because they identify products of operators of the model that are equal to identity in the system space). Gauge transformations are changes of basis of the state space of the system, which, in this framework, correspond to automorphisms of the codomain V = ( Z/q Z)n (see Fig. 7). These automorphisms can be represented by invertible n × n-matrices over (Z/q Z) and the GT of a model is then obtained by the matrix product previously described in property 22. 

Definition 23. Gauge transformations. GTs are automorphisms of the codomain of the linear map fM

Figure 7: Illustration: Representation of q-state spin models as linear maps. We consider a system with n q -state spin variables and a spin model M with K operators. The space V = ( Z/q Z)n (on the right) is the system space, in which each of the n basis elements is associated with one of the spin variables. The state of the system is then represented by the random vector α = ( α1, . . . , α n) ∈ V , where αj is the color of the 

j-th spin, and a spin operator is represented as a vector μ ∈ V , which gives the decomposition of the operator in terms of the variables of the system. The space U = ( Z/q Z)K (on the left) is the model space, in which each of the K basis elements is associated with one of the operators of the model (which are the variables of the models). The state of the model is then represented by the random vector m = ( m1, . . . , m K ) ∈ U ,where mk is the color of the k-th operator, ϕuk (m) = zmk

q , and each spin operator that can be decomposed over the operators of the model is represented by a vector u ∈ U . A model M is represented by a linear map 

fM that associates each operator represented by u ∈ U in model space to a representation μ = fM(u) ∈ V 

in system space. The map fM is defined in Eq. (106) by specifying the image μk = fM(uk) ∈ V of each basis vector uk ∈ U . Gauge transformations T are automorphisms of V. Loops of M correspond to the vectors ℓ ∈ U whose image is the null vector fM(ℓ) = 0 ∈ V .Using this framework, we can identify several properties of linear maps that are useful for characterizing spin models, such as the rank of the map and the properties of its kernel. The notion of rank of a spin model M was already introduced in Definition 14 as the cardinality of the largest linearly independent set of operators in M. This is the same as the rank of the matrix representation of M, and therefore it coincides with the rank of the linear map associated with M. The kernel of a linear map is the set of all the vectors in the domain that are mapped to the zero vector in the codomain (as illustrated by the vector ℓ in Fig. 7). For example, the vector u1 + u3 + 2 u5 is in the kernel of the map fM defined in Eq. (107), as its image is the zero vector: 

fM(u1 + u3 + 2 u5) = μ1 + μ3 + 2 μ5 = v1 + v2 + 2( v1 + v2) = 0 . (108) Here, we can recognize one of the loops of M, which is the first loop represented in Fig. 6.b (top left loop). We recall that a loop of a model with operators {μk}1≤k≤K is a vector ℓ ∈ (Z/q Z)K that verifies that 

∑Kk=1 ℓkμk = 0, where ℓk denotes the k-th coefficient of ℓ (see Eq. (58)). Labeling the operators in the 42 arXiv Template A Preprint 

same order as in Eq. (107), the loop above can be represented as the coordinate vector ℓ = u1 + u3 + 2 u5 =(1 , 0, 1, 0, 2, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0) in U. More generally, the loops of a model M are precisely the elements of the kernel of fM; they identify dependence relations in V between the operators μk of M, i.e., between the images of the basis elements uk. Let’s provide a more formal proof of this last point.

Property 25. Spin models M inherit properties of their linear map representation fM in Def. 22: – the matrix representation of a model in Definition 20 is the matrix representation of the map; – the rank of a model in Definition 14 is the rank of the map, rank( M) = rank( fM);– the set of loops of a model defined in Eq. (58) is the kernel of the map, Lq (M) = ker( fM).

Figure 6.b shows the loops of the spin model M taken as an example above. In this model, operators are included by conjugate pairs, just like for real-valued spin models (see Definition 5). For clarity in Fig. 6, we only displayed the elements of the reduced set of loops Lcc q (M) defined for real-valued spin models in Eq. (68); in short, we didn’t represent trivial loops resulting from complex conjugate operators (of the type 

μi + μj = 0) nor their combinations with other loops. For example, the first loop in the top left of the figure corresponds to μ1 + μ3 + 2 μ5 = v1 + v2 + 2( v1 + v2) = 0 in (Z/3Z)n. Considering all linear combinations of this loop with the trivial loop μ1 + μ2 = 0 from the c.c. operators μ1 and μ2, we also get the following loops: 2μ1 + μ2 + μ3 + 2 μ5 = 0, 2μ2 + μ3 + 2 μ5 = 0, and μ2 + 2 μ3 + μ5 = 0, which are not represented in Fig. 6.b. Note that the choice of the loops in Lcc q (M) is not unique, but it is fully determined by the choice of the operators in the reduced model representation Mr .The reduced loop structure Lcc q (M) of a real-valued spin model can be also related to the kernel of the model’s reduced representation (see Definition 21). More precisely, for real-valued spin models that can be written under the form 24 M = M1 ∪ M ∗ 

> 1

with the decomposition of Definition 10 (i.e., for which M0 = ∅ –such as the model taken as an example above), the reduced set of loops of M, excluding trivial loops due to c.c. operators, is equal to the kernel of its reduced matrix representation M 1: Lcc q (M) = Lq (M1) = ker( M 1),where M 1 is a matrix whose columns are the operators in M1 (see proof in App. C.3). Finally, because Lq (M) = ker( fM), the loop structure of a spin model inherits the properties of kernels of linear maps. In particular if q is prime, the kernel of fM is a vector subspace of U = ( Z/q Z)K of dimension 

dim(ker( fM)) = K −rank( fM), where K = |M| ; we denote this dimension λ. Consequently, all the elements of ker( fM) can be generated by a set of λ linearly independent vectors in ker( fM), and the cardinality of 

ker( fM) is then qλ. This generalizes to q > 2 and prime the result found in the binary case [15] that the set of loops of a binary spin model has the structure of an Abelian group generated by λ independent loops, where λ = K − rank( M) with K = |M| .

Property 26. For q prime, the set of loops Lq (M) of a spin model M with K = |M| operators is a vector subspace of (Z/q Z)K of dimension λ = K − rank( M). It is generated by λ independent loops, and its cardinality is |L q (M)| = qλ.

We saw in Sec. 3 that the loop expansion of the partition function ZM of spin models makes a connection between the statistical properties of a model (captured by ZM) and its algebraic properties corresponding to linear dependencies between its operators (captured by the loop structure). The framework introduced here formalizes the latter as the kernel of the linear map representing the model, thus providing a formal algebraic characterization of the statistical properties of q-state spin models. 

4.6 Equivalence classes of spin models, and their statistical and algebraic properties 

In the binary case ( q = 2 ), Ref. [15] introduces the notion of equivalence classes of spin models, as classes of models related by GTs. They found that these models have the same partition function and thus define the same statistical structure, only observed in different bases. In other words, models of the same class can be seen as different representations of the same abstract statistical model. In particular, they share common statistical and algebraic properties: they have the same loop structure, partition function, and model information-theoretic complexity. Ref. [15] calls these classes “complexity classes”. Let us discuss these results in the more general context of q-state spin models with q ≥ 2.Consider a real-valued q-state spin model M. A gauge transformation Tb of M is a bijection of the state space that rearranges the energy landscape of the model, assigning the energy of each state s in M to another (unique) state s′ = b(s) in the transformed model M′ = Tb[M], as described in Definition 19. As a result, the probability 25 of finding the system in the state s in the model M is equal to the probability of finding the system in the transformed state s′ = b(s) in the transformed model M′ = Tb[M]:

p(s | M , g) = p(s′ | M ′, g′) . (111) Here, the vector of parameters g′ = Tb[g] is a permutation of the parameters g of M such that each transformed operator ϕμ′

= Tb[ϕμ] in M′ is parameterized by the same parameter as the operator ϕμ in M

(see definition 19). In equation (111), the reassignment in state space created by the GT shuffles the state probabilities in the model, but preserves the overall shape of the distribution. In other words, Eq. (111) expresses that the probability distributions of model M and of its gauge transformation M′ are the same up to permutation of the states. More precisely, one can verify that the transformed spin model M′ has the same statistical properties as the original model M by comparing their partition functions. The partition function (3) of a spin model is obtained by summing, over all possible states s of the spin system, a term that depends only on the energy of the state (exponential of minus the energy of the state). This sum thus remains unchanged under the one-to-one state reordering of the energy landscape created by a GT (see proof in App. C.2, which is also valid for spin models with complex-valued Hamiltonian and partition function). In other words, just like        

> 25 We recall that for real-valued spin models, the probability to observe a state sis proportional to exponential minus the energy H(s)of the state: p(s)∝exp( −H(s)) .

44 arXiv Template A Preprint 

in the binary case [15], q-state spin models related by GTs have the same partition function. Because the partition function encapsulates all the statistical properties of a spin model, this means that models related by GTs describe the same statistical structure, only represented in different bases. From a statistical modeling perspective, the Fisher information matrix (when it is defined) is in general the mathematical object that captures all the statistical properties of a parametric statistical model (not just models that have a partition function, such as spin models). In particular, in the context of information geometry, it can be used as a metric for the model manifold to study the statistical properties of the model [43, 44]. For spin models, the Fisher information matrix is given by the Hessian of the log-partition function: 

[ IM(g) ] μ,ν = − 〈∂gμ ∂gν log p(s | M , g)〉 

> p(s|g,M)

= ∂gμ ∂gν log ZM(g) , (112) and is therefore identical for models related by GTs (assuming the parameters are labeled consistently between the models, as explained for Eq. (111)). This means that spin models related by GTs correspond to the same statistical manifold (up to permutation of the parameters). Therefore, also from this geometrical perspective, models related by GTs are just different representations of the same abstract statistical model. 

Property 27. Statistical properties of spin models invariant under GTs. q-state spin models related by gauge transformations have the same partition function (for a model M and GT T , ZM(g) = ZT [M](g) ). For real-valued spin models, which can be used as probabilistic models in the context of statistical inference, this implies that they have the same Fisher information matrix. In other words, they describe the same statistical structure represented in different spin bases. 

More generally, the invariance of the partition function under gauge transformations is valid for all q-state spin models (not just real-valued models). This is straightforward to see using the loop expansion of the partition function in Sec. 3 and the linear algebra framework introduced previously, in which a spin model 

M is associated to a linear map fM. Based on the loop expansion (57), the partition of a spin model is fully specified by its number of operators (each parameterized by a different parameter) and its loop structure. The number of operators of a model is invariant under GT (by definition 19), while the loop structure has been identified as the kernel of the linear map, Lq (M) = ker( fM). Because a GT is an automorphism of the co-domain of fM (change of basis of the space of state variables – see Fig. 7), it has no effect on the kernel of fM. This can be written down explicitly using the matrix representations M of fM (and of the model M) and T of a given GT (which is an n × n invertible matrix in (Z/q Z)). The transformed model is represented by the matrix M ′ = T −1M and its loop structure is given by: 

Lq (M′) = 

{

ℓ ∈ (Z/q Z)K

∣∣∣ T −1M ℓ = 0

}

=

{

ℓ ∈ (Z/q Z)K

∣∣∣ M ℓ = T 0

}

= Lq (M) ,

where we multiplied by the matrix T on both sides of the equation defining the set. In other words, a spin model and its gauge transformation have the same loop structure, and therefore we recover that they have the same partition function. In doing so, we also identify an algebraic property of spin models that is invariant under GT, namely their loop structure. Moreover, the rank and the dimension of a spin model, which are respectively the cardinality of the maximal set of independent operators within the model, and the cardinality of the minimal independent set of operators that can generate all the operators of the model, are also invariant under GT. 

Property 28. Algebraic properties of spin models invariant under GTs. The number of op-erators, the rank, the dimension, and the loop structure Lq of a q-state spin model are invariant un-der gauge transformations. For any q-state spin model M and gauge transformation T , |T [M]| = |M| ,

rank( T [M]) = rank( M), and Lq (T [M]) = Lq (M).

For example, take the top left loop of model M in Fig. 6.b; this loop corresponds to the vector ℓ =

u1 + u3 + 2 u5, whose image by fM is: 

fM(u1 + u3 + 2 u5) = μ1 + μ3 + 2 μ5 = v1 + v2 + 2( v1 + v2) = 0 , (113) where the sums on the right-hand side are taken in (Z/3Z)n. For the transformed model M′ = T [M], the image of the same vector ℓ by the linear map fM′ associated with M′ is also the null vector:

In particular, ℓ, which was a loop of M, remains a loop of M′.Like in the binary case [15], GTs define an equivalence relation between q-state spin models (one can easily check for reflexivity, symmetry, transitivity 26 ). This equivalence relation divides the (superexponential) space of the 2qn−1 spin models into equivalence classes of models that share common statistical and algebraic properties. In section 5, we will focus on the subset of classes corresponding to minimally complex models [23]. 

Property 29. Equivalence classes of q-state spin models and their properties. Gauge transforma-tions define an equivalence relation between q-state spin models, dividing the space of models into equivalence classes. Models of the same class are different representations of the same abstract statistical model. Each class is thus characterized by the statistical and algebraic invariants given in Property 27 and 28. 

4.7 Discussion: equivariant modeling and learning symmetries of data under gauge transformations 

The gauge transformation of a discrete dataset simply re-writes the data in a different basis, without any loss or added information or noise. Ideally, a perfect model selection procedure would be able to find models that are consistent for the original data and its transformed version, as illustrated in Figure 1 in the introduction. In other words, this ideal model selection would be equivariant under GTs. Such approach is theoretically possible if we select among all q-state spin models, because this family of models is invariant under GTs (for any given q). However, brute force selection of the best model among all is impossible in practice, due to the huge set of models (super-exponential in the number of spins). In Section 5, we extend to discrete data the work of Ref. [23] on minimally complex models, which are a sub-family of spin models within which one can perform equivariant model selection in practice. More generally, if one wants to tackle model selection among all q-state spin models, it is important to find principled ways to reduce the number of parameters and the set of possible models for a given dataset. One approach is to focus on modeling symmetries of the data, instead of specific correlation patterns. If a dataset is invariant (up to noise) under certain bijective transformations, then a good model should encode that invariance, i.e., it should be invariant under the same transformation. This reduces the set of candidate models. In other words, we simplify the problem of identifying relevant correlation patterns of the data by searching first for relevant symmetries of the data. Indeed, it is in general difficult to identify in a robust manner the set of the most relevant (high-order) correlation patterns of a dataset, because these patterns are typically not independent from each other and, as a result, many combinations of possible relevant patterns can explain the data similarly 27 . In contrast, one can treat symmetries of the data independently from each other, and each newly uncovered symmetry further reduces the pool of possible candidate models. In the following, we formalize this idea based on the notion of invariance under GTs. As discussed in Sec. 4.2, applying a GT to a dataset permutes the probability of occurrence of the states in the empirical distribution. However, certain GTs can leave the empirical distribution fully invariant, by only permuting states that have the same probability. Such invariance can be interpreted as a symmetry of the data under GT. The definition of GTs thus allows us to formalize the notion of generalized symmetries in discrete data, as the invariance of the empirical probability distribution under GT. These symmetries go beyond ordinary symmetries, such as translational and reflection symmetries for spatially organized variables, or symmetries under permutations of spin variables. 

Definition 24. Generalized symmetry of (stationary) discrete data. We call symmetry of a discrete dataset the invariance of its empirical distribution under a gauge transformation.                                   

> 26 Reflexivity: a spin model is a GT of itself, where the GT is the identity. Symmetry: consider a model M′
> obtained by the GT Tof another model M, then Mis also the gauge transformation of M′using T−1. Transitivity: consider two GTs T1and T2, and the transformed model M′=T1[M]and M′′ =T2[M′], then M′′ =T[M]where
> T=T2[T1]is also a GT resulting from the composition of T1and T2.
> 27 This problem is in fact equivalent to identifying the best spin model for the data, by definition of maximum entropy models [1].

46 arXiv Template A Preprint 

For q-state spin models, gauge transformations are changes of representation of the models. They transform a model’s architecture (i.e., which operators are in the model), while preserving its statistical and algebraic properties (e.g., partition function and loop structure). However, certain GTs can leave a given model architecture fully invariant, by transforming each operator of the model into a spin operator that is already in the model. In other words, a model M and its transformation T [M] have the same set of operators and thus correspond to the same parametric model. For example, take Bob’s model in the introduction Figure 1. The permutation of the spins s1 and s2 is a GT that leaves the model’s architecture invariant. Equivalently, for Alice’s model, the GT (σ′

> 1

, σ ′

> 2

, σ ′

> 3

) = ( σ1, σ 1σ2σ3, σ 3) leaves the model invariant (as it permutes the field operator σ2 with the three-body operator σ1σ2σ3). We call such invariance of the model architecture under GT a symmetry of the spin model. Contrary to the definition 24 of symmetry of data, the symmetry of a model under GT doesn’t necessarily leave the model probability distribution invariant, because the parameters associated with the permuted operators are then also permuted. For the model probability distribution to remain invariant for any value of the parameters, the parameters of the permuted operators must be equal (degenerate parameters). 

Definition 25. Generalized symmetry of q-state spin models. We call symmetry of a spin model the invariance of its architecture (which operators are in the model) under a gauge transformation. We call “strong” symmetry of a spin model the invariance of the model probability distribution under a GT. Such invariance requires both an invariance of the model architecture and the degeneracy of the model parameters associated with the operators permuted by the GT. 

We can thus exploit generalized symmetries of a discrete dataset to reduce the pool of candidate spin models to those that have the same symmetries. An invariance of the data under a GT should be reflected in the candidate models as a strong symmetry, which imposes a degeneracy of the parameters associated with the operators permuted by the GT 28 : they must either all be absent from the model or all have the same parameter. The model selection procedure should then decide if this degenerate parameter is relevant or if these operators should be all be removed from the model 29 .For real data, the probabilities of occurrence of the states are known up to some uncertainty that depends on the size of the data: the larger the number of datapoints, the smaller the uncertainty. To find all the symmetries of a finite dataset, one should take into account the level of uncertainty (even more so in undersampling regimes). This can be done by reducing the noise in the empirical probability distribution, for instance, by assigning the same probability to states that have similar frequencies at the level of precision warranted by the data (e.g., following the procedure of [45]). A smaller number of different relevant probabilities in the empirical distribution increases the number of symmetries observed in the data, and therefore reduces the number of parameters in the model. This is a consequence of the duality between mixture models and models of the exponential family [40], which was exploited for instance for model reduction and parameter fitting for small systems in [46]. Such a procedure can significantly reduce the number of possible model parameters. A model selection procedure would then have fewer parameters to select from to identify if they are relevant or not. These parameters would also be associated with larger “macro”-patterns (composed of multiple operators). Such an approach has the potential to greatly simplify the model selection procedure and lead to more robust model discovery.      

> 28 For example, take a binary dataset with 3spins that is invariant under the GT characterized by the inverse transformation T−1=

(1 0 01 1 00 1 1

)                                                                      

> . Then the operators (100) ,(110) ,(101) ,(111) should be given the same parameters as well as the operators (011) and (010) for the model to be strongly symmetric under the same transformation (this can be checked by multiplying the matrix T−1with each of these operators as in Eq. (96)).
> 29 Note that a symmetry under GT will not reduce the set of models to a specific subset of equivalence classes, but instead it will invalidate candidate models from all classes. A symmetry under a given GT is indeed representation-dependent. For each transformation under which a model Mis invariant, there is a corresponding transformation under which another model of the same class is invariant. Consider a transformation T1under which M1is invariant:
> T1M1=M1.Consider another model M2of the same class: there exists a GT Tsuch that M2=T M 1.The model M2is then invariant under the GT T2=T T 1T−1, as it satisfies that T2M2=M2. Furthermore, if Tand
> T1commute, then T2=T1, which means that the models M1and M2are invariant under the same GT T1.

5 Limiting the search to Minimally Complex Models (MCMs) 

Despite the existence of equivalent models, the space of models is still extremely large, making it difficult to perform model selection. Ref. [23] proposes to perform model selection among a sub-family of spin models that they call Minimally Complex Models (MCMs), for which all the quantities of interest in the context of statistical inference are computationally cheap to calculate, making model selection feasible in practice among these models. In particular, Ref. [23] introduced MCMs as an alternative to pairwise models that respects the structure of gauge transformations (GTs), because the family of MCMs is invariant under GT (the GT of an MCM is also an MCM). This is not the case for families of spin models restricted to have interactions only up to a certain order, such as pairwise models. Working with MCMs thus brings a new perspective to statistical modeling, by making it possible to tackle data modeling problems in a way that is independent of the basis in which the data was originally represented. This is interesting especially for systems where it is not clear if the current representation of the data is the most meaningful, or when one is interested in tackling a modeling problem from the most general perspective, in the absence of any information about the system. Model selection with MCMs is also efficient thanks to the peculiar interaction structure of MCMs, in which interactions are grouped into independent sub-systems. More precisely, in some representations, an MCM corresponds to a factorization of the model multivariate probability distribution over subsets of basis variables; i.e., the model assumes that these subsets are statistically independent from each other (see Fig. 8). Performing model selection among MCMs thus aims to uncover the factorization of the model probability distribution that best matches the structure of the data. Such factorization reduces the dimensionality of the statistical inference problem, while preserving most of the relevant information within the data. MCMs can thus be used to identify groups of variables that carry most of the multivariate information in the data, or to facilitate the modeling problem for large systems by breaking it down into lower-dimensional ones. Ref. [39] likens this approach to performing community detection for data (by analogy to community detection in network analysis), giving a simple interpretation to MCMs in terms of community structure. In this section, we focus on a subset of q-state spin models that generalize the framework of minimally complex models [23] to discrete systems. 

5.1 Definitions 

Minimally complex models (MCMs) are defined in the context of binary data as models constructed from the union of independent complete components (ICCs) [23]. Here, we generalize to the discrete case the def-initions of complete components (CCs), independent components (ICs), and MCMs. We call a “component” of a spin model M any subset of operators of M.For q prime, we can directly extend to q > 2 the definition of complete components from Ref. [23]. 

Definition 26. Complete Component (CC), for q prime. A complete component is a set of operators 

Ma ⊆ [( Z/q Z)n]∗ that verifies that, for any two operators (μ, ν) ∈ M 2 

> a

, all the operators obtained by linear combinations of μ and ν in (Z/q Z)n are in Ma ∪ 0 , i.e.: 

∀(μ, ν) ∈ M 2 

> a

, ∀(c, d ) ∈ (Z/q Z)2, c μ + d ν ∈ M a ∪ 0 . (115) 

In other words, Ma ∪ 0 is a vector subspace of (Z/q Z)n of dimension ra, where ra is the cardinality of the largest linearly independent set in Ma (namely the rank of Ma – see Definition 14). 

In this definition, the set Ma of operators can be a subset of operators of a model (hence the use of the term “component” instead of “model”). We recall that the interaction vector c μ + d ν is associated with the spin operator ϕc μ+d ν (α) .

= [ ϕμ(α)] c [ϕν (α)] d. For q > 2, because of conjugate operators, the vector c μ + d ν

can be equal to 0 even for (c, d )̸ = (0 , 0) , which is why we added the element 0 to Ma in Eq. (115). This doesn’t happen in the binary case [23]. Extending Definition 26 to non-prime values of q would define as being “complete” model components that we don’t want to consider complete in the context of MCMs. For example, take the component Ma = {(2 , 2) }

MCM 

Figure 8: (colors online) Visualization of MCMs as factorization in state space. Left. In each row, the left panel shows an MCM based on four discrete variables (α1, α 2, α 3, α 4) with two ICCs represented in different colors. The MCM is represented in a preferred basis, which means that each ICC contains all possible operators over the shown subset of α-variables. The value of q for the discrete variables is not yet specified and the diagrams can be read for any chosen value of q. For example, for the top row: for 

q = 2 , the red ICC has the q2 − 1 = 3 operators (μ1, μ 2) ∈ { (1 , 0) , (0 , 1) , (1 , 1) }; for q = 3 , the red ICC has the q2 − 1 = 8 operators (μ1, μ 2) ∈ { (1 , 0) , (2 , 0) , (0 , 1) , (0 , 2) , (1 , 1) , (2 , 2) , (1 , 2) , (2 , 1) }. Right. The panel represents the factorization in state space (for q = 2 and q = 3 ) that results from the MCM partition of the basis variables αi shown in the left panel. The model probability distribution can be written as the product of joint distributions over lower dimensional subspaces shown in colors (line, surface, or volume). The color (green or red) refers to the corresponding ICC in the left panel and each marker represents a state of the system reduced to these subspaces (e.g., for the top panel and q = 2 , the pair of variables (α1, α 2) can take the values {(0 , 0) , (1 , 0) , (0 , 1) , (1 , 1) }, hence the four markers in the red area). The probability of the states that are not represented by a colored marker is obtained by products of the probabilities of the sub-states that are represented by a marker. When written in a preferred basis, the factorization of the state space resulting from the same MCM partition of the basis variables into ICC (as in the left panel) doesn’t depend on the value of the discretization q of the variables. The only difference is the increase of the number of states in each subspace with q.with a single pairwise interaction for n = 2 spins and q = 4 . This component satisfies Eq. (115), because the only multiples of the operator (2 , 2) in (Z/4Z)2 are (0 , 0) and itself, which are both in Ma ∪ 0. The component Ma is in some sense complete, but it is isomorphic to Z/2Z and not to Z/q Z (here q = 4 ). Considering such component as complete is problematic, because an MCM would then correspond to a factorization over “CC” defined for variables of different types (i.e., with different values of q). We would also lose the re-parametrization property of CC (see Property 40 below), which is necessary for the efficient computation of many quantities of interest for modeling data with MCMs (see Sec. 5.4). To exclude this possibility, we give the following general definition of complete components, valid for any integer value of q.

Definition 27. (for all q) Complete Component (CC). A set of operators is said to be complete if it is equal to the set of all the operators generated by an independent set of operators (see Definition 11 of independent operators). Formally, consider a set of operators Ma ⊆ [( Z/q Z)n]∗ and let I = {μ1, . . . , μra }

be a maximal independent set of operators in Ma. The model component Ma is said complete if its elements

where we used the notation ∗ to indicate that we exclude the null vector (which doesn’t correspond to an operator). In other words, Ma ∪ 0 is a submodule of (Z/q Z)n isomorphic to (Z/q Z)ra , where ra is the cardinality of I (namely, the rank of Ma, denoted rank( Ma)). 

From this definition, it follows that the number of operators in a complete component of rank ra is equal to the number of nonzero vectors in (Z/q Z)ra , which is qra − 1.

Property 30. Number of operators in a CC. The number of operators in a complete component Ma

is |M a| = qra − 1, where ra denotes the rank of Ma.

We can then directly extend the definition of independence between complete components from the binary case to the more general discrete case. 

Definition 28. Independence between CC and Minimally Complex Models (MCMs). A set of complete components {M a}a∈A is said to be independent if none of the operators of a given component Ma

can be obtained by linear combinations of operators of the other components (i.e., generated by the set of operators ∪a′∈A\ aMa′ ). This then leads to the definition of Minimally Complex Models (MCMs) as models formed by the union of independent complete components (ICCs), M = ∪a∈A Ma.

In the following, we give a more general definition of independence between components, for components that are not necessarily complete. Our goal is to build a general formalism that can be used for any q-state spin model (not just MCMs) and which allows for modeling data in a basis independent way. For general spin models, the notion of independence between components is directly linked to a factorization property of the model probability distribution (see Property 39 below); this factorization can be in the original representation of the data or in another gauge-transformed basis. In this framework, MCMs are a natural stepping stone towards modeling the data with more complex spin models. To construct this formalism, we first introduce the definition of basis of a component , which we will then use to define independent components. 

Definition 29. Basis and dimension of a component. A basis of a component Ma, denoted BMa , is a minimal set of da independent operators 30 that can generate all the operators of Ma:

BMa = ( μ1, · · · , μda ) ⊆ [( Z/q Z)n]∗ such that Ma ⊆ ⟨ BMa ⟩gen . (117) 

We call the cardinality da of such basis the dimension of the component and denote it dim( Ma). By definition, in an n-spin system, dim( Ma) ≤ n for any model component Ma. In the following, we consider the operators of the basis to be ordered, which we indicated in Eq. (117) by using parentheses instead of curly brackets in 

(μ1, · · · , μda ) and a bold symbol for BMa .This definition is also valid if the component coincides with the whole model, thus defining the basis and the dimension of a q-state spin model. 

The basis of a component is not necessarily unique, but its dimension is (by definition). Note the difference between the rank and the dimension of a component: the rank of a component is the cardinality of the largest independent set within the component, whereas its dimension is the cardinality of the smallest independent set containing the component. If q is prime, the dimension and the rank of a component are always equal, but this is not necessarily the case if q is not prime, for which the dimension can be larger than the rank 31 . However for CC, any maximal independent set of operators in the component can generate the whole component, by definition 27 of completeness. This means that such a set is also a basis of the CC and therefore that the dimension of a CC is equal to its rank for all values of q. In general, q-state spin models

Property 31. Basis, dimension, and rank of a CC. Any maximal independent set of operators in a CC is also a basis of the component. Consequently, the dimension and the rank of a CC are equal, for all integer values of q (prime or not). In the following, we will denote by ra = rank( Ma) = dim( Ma) both the rank and the dimension of a CC Ma.

We have already introduced a definition of spin basis in Sec. 4, in the context of the definition 17 of gauge transformations (GTs). We defined a spin basis for an n-spin system as an ordered set of n independent operators, denoted b = ( ϕμ1 , · · · , ϕ μn ), and a GT as a change of spin basis of the form T : s → s′ =

b(s). A basis of a component in Definition 29 also corresponds to a spin basis, but it is reduced to the (minimal) subspace in which the component is defined. Note that, in Definition 29 and throughout the paper, we used the term “operator” for both the spin operator ϕμ (proper use of the term) and its vector representation μ. For clarity and coherence of the notations, we will denote a basis of a component Ma by 

bMa = ( ϕμ1 , · · · , ϕ μda ) when written in terms of the spin operators ϕμ and by BMa = ( μ1, · · · , μda ) when written in terms of their vector representation μ.Using the definition of basis of a component, we now define independent components. 

Definition 30. Independent Components (ICs). Consider a set of operators M ⊆ [( Z/q Z)n]∗ formed by the union of disjoint components Ma : M = ∪a∈A Ma, where A is a set of labels identifying the com-ponents. Consider a basis BMa for each component Ma. The set of components {M a}a∈A is said to be independent if, for each component Ma, none of the operators generated by BMa can be generated by the combined bases of the other components (except for the null vector):

where ⟨S⟩gen denotes the set of all the operators generated by a set S of operators over (Z/q Z)n. In other words, the bases BMa for all a ∈ A generate independent subspaces of (Z/q Z)n (each isomorphic to (Z/q Z)da ,where da = dim( Ma)), and the operators of each component Ma belong only to the subspace defined by BMa .

For example, any choice of decomposition of an independent model (see Definition 16) into components gives a set of independent components. As a direct consequence of the independence between the components Ma, a ∈A , one can construct a basis BM of a model M = ∪a∈A Ma by combining the elements of the basis BMa of each component. The resulting set of operators, BM = ∪a∈A BMa , is indeed still independent, thanks to Eq. (118). This is generally not the case for random choices of decomposition of M in components Ma, as the components can belong to overlapping (minimal) subspaces of (Z/q Z)n. We recall that a basis was defined as an ordered set; here we assume that the elements in A have an order, and we create the combined basis BM by “gluing” together the operators of the basis-component BMa in that order (same for the notation bM = ∪a∈A bMa

that will also be used below). In practice, the specific choice of ordering of these basis components doesn’t matter, but it must be consistent throughout the analysis. 

Property 32. Basis and dimension of ICs. Consider a model M formed by the union of components, 

M = ∪a∈A Ma, and consider a basis BMa for each component. If the components Ma are independent, then the set of operators B = ∪a∈A BMa forms a basis of M, and the dimension of M is thus equal to the sum of the dimensions of the ICs Ma, dim( M) = ∑ 

> a∈A

dim( Ma).

Finally, we recover the definition 28 of minimally complex models by combining the definitions of complete components and independent components 32 .

Definition 31. Minimally Complex Models (MCMs). A q-state spin model is a minimally complex model if its set of operators, M ⊆ [( Z/q Z)n]∗, is formed by the union of independent complete compo-nents (ICCs) Ma:

M = ⋃

> a∈A

Ma , (119) 

where A is a set of labels identifying the ICCs. In other words, {M a}a∈A is a set of independent components and, for all a ∈ A , the component Ma is complete. Each ICC thus completely models an independent subspace of (Z/q Z)n.

Note that contrary to the binary case, an independent model, as defined in Definition 13, is not an MCM. This is because each independent subspace of dimension da = 1 requires (q − 1) operators to be fully modeled by a q-state spin model, while an independent model only has one operator per dimension (which is strictly less than (q − 1) for q > 2). However, we can define a notion of independent MCM in the discrete case, which reduces to that of independent model in the binary case. 

Definition 32. Independent Minimally Complex Models (IMCMs). MCMs for which each ICC has rank 1. 

These models will be useful for the search algorithms for the best MCM proposed in Sec. 6.1. Finally, MCMs inherit all the properties of CCs and ICs mentioned above. 

Property 33. Number of operators, basis, dimension, and rank of an MCM. Consider an MCM M = ∪a∈A Ma with ICCs {M a}a∈A . As a consequence of the completeness of the components, the dimension and the rank of each component Ma are equal (see Property 31), denoted ra = rank( Ma) = dim( Ma), and the number of operators in M is given by (see Property 30): 

|M| = ∑

> a∈A

(qra − 1) . (120) 

Thanks to the independence between the components Ma, one can obtain a basis of M by combining the bases BMa of its components (see Property 32): 

BM = ∪a∈A BMa . (121) 

The dimension of M is thus equal to the sum of the dimensions of its ICCs Ma, and so is its rank: rank( M) = dim( M) = ∑ 

> a∈A

ra. In the following, we will denote by r both the rank and the dimension of an MCM M.

Figure 9 gives four examples of MCMs. Observe that in some representations, the complete components 

Ma of an MCMs can spread over more than ra = rank( Ma) variables. For example, the two models in panel (b) both have a single ICC of rank ra = 1 . In the top model, this ICC spreads over two variables (with pairwise operators), whereas in the bottom model, the ICC is compact, based solely on one variable, and we can immediately see that the component is complete and of rank 1. Similarly, the two models in panel (a) both have two ICCs of rank ra = 1 and 2 respectively. It is not easy to see that the top model is an MCM, because the ICC of rank 2 is spread over three variables. In contrast in the bottom model, one can clearly identify the two ICCs, with all possible operators based on σ1 and σ2 for one ICC, and all the operators based on σ3 for the other ICC. In these examples, the top and bottom models of each panel are related by gauge transformations, which means that they are different representations of the same abstract MCM (see sec. 4). In the following section, we discuss GTs of MCMs. 

5.2 Gauge transformations of MCMs, equivalence classes, and counting 

Similarly to the binary case, the family of MCMs for q > 2 is invariant under gauge transformations (GTs), meaning that the GT of an MCM is also an MCM (see Def. 17 for the definition of GTs). Indeed, under a GT, a complete component remains complete and with the same rank, and a set of independent components remains independent (see App. D.2). This is an interesting property of MCMs if one wants to tackle model selection of discrete data from a more general perspective, independently of the basis in which the data is originally represented. In the following, we lay out the foundations to work with MCMs in a basis-independent way. 52 arXiv Template A Preprint <latexit sha1_base64="jcKgyncInR38An1qtYCoafaT8pY=">AAAD2nicrVNNb9NAEN3YfBTzlcKRy4qoEkIliu2qVEWVKrhwQSpS01TKJtZ6s0lXXa/N7rpSZPnCAYS48su48S/4Cazt4NROuTGSrfG8mXnzRuMw4UzpweBXx7Jv3b5zd+uec//Bw0ePu9tPzlScSkKHJOaxPA+xopwJOtRMc3qeSIqjkNNRePmuwEdXVCoWi1O9TOgkwgvB5oxgbULBduf3DoqwviCYZx/ywJ1mL1EiWURzZw2c5mj30HyHdMFERgydMjCE6A1SbBHhwIVHUJm3CjyIUAPyasifZmjXy1u4X+K+6U7FrO7tXOOeZq/c/NBpshcdVMlb89QNDUMFe2vYrfOqKerseqiqxF+X+E5jpE9He/95JS3Nm5Jvat8Wjf6G/ynWzxtEQbc36A9Kg5uOu3J6YGUnQfcnmsUkjajQhGOlxu4g0ZMMS80IN3eCUkUTTC7xgo6NK3BE1SQrTzOHOyYyg/NYmkdoWEavV2Q4UmoZhSazkK/aWBG8CRunen4wyZhIUk0FqYjmKYc6hsWdwxmTlGi+NA4mkplZIbnAEhNt/gbHLMFtS950zry+u9/f/7jXO367WscWeAaegxfABa/BMXgPTsAQEGtk5dYX66uN7M/2N/t7lWp1VjVPQcPsH38Aq8YstQ==</latexit>

Property 34. Gauge transformation of an MCM. The gauge transformation of an MCM is also an MCM and has ICCs of the same ranks as the original model. 

Figure 9 shows examples of GTs of MCMs. In particular, it highlights the transformation of MCMs into a preferred basis , a useful notion introduced in the binary case [23]. As noted previously, in some representations, a complete component Ma can spread over more than ra = rank( Ma) basis variables. For example, the top model for q = 4 in Fig. 9.b has a single CC M = {(1 , 1) , (2 , 2) , (3 , 3) } with operators of order two despite having only a rank r = 1 . Using the GT σ1 = s1s2 and σ2 = s1, M is transformed into the model M′ = {(1 , 0) , (2 , 0) , (3 , 0) } (see App. D.1), in which all the operators are based solely on r = 1 

variable (see Fig. 9.b bottom). Ref. [23] introduced the notion of preferred bases of an MCM, which are spin bases in which each ICC Ma of the MCM appears in a compact form, based solely on ra = rank( Ma) basis elements just like for M′ above. The model in Fig. 9.a bottom is another example of an MCM represented in a preferred basis. 

Definition 33. Preferred basis of an MCM. A preferred basis of an MCM is a spin basis, b∗ =(ϕμ1 , · · · , ϕ μn ), in which each ICC Ma is based solely on ra basis elements, where ra is the rank (and dimension) of Ma. In such basis, each ICC appears in a compact form and the MCM corresponds to a partition of a subset of the basis variables in which each part identifies an ICC (see dashed outlines in Fig. 9 bottom – the variables are fully connected within each part, with operators of all orders, and not connected between the parts). 

53 arXiv Template A Preprint 

In other words, a preferred spin basis b∗ for an MCM defines a GT T : s → s′ = b∗(s) (see Definition 17) that transforms the MCM into its compact representation, with each ICC based on a minimal number of basis variables. More generally, one can define a preferred basis for any set of ICs (for which the components are not necessarily complete) as a basis in which each component Ma is only expressed over da = dim( Ma)

basis variables. It is always possible to construct a preferred basis for an MCM M = ∪a∈A Ma (and more generally for a set of ICs), simply by combining the elements of a basis bMa for each IC Ma, thanks to the independence between the ICCs (see Property 32). We obtain a spin basis of the form b∗ = ∪a∈A bMa ∪ bM , where 

bM is a set of (n − dim( M)) independent operators chosen to complete the spin basis. In such a basis, the operators of each component Ma would be based solely on their respective basis variables bMa (by definition 30 of ICs), which is precisely what we want for a preferred basis. Moreover, for MCMs, because the component Ma are complete, we can choose any set of ra = rank( Ma) independent operators in Ma to define its basis bMa = ( ϕμa 

> 1

, · · · , ϕ μara ) (see Property 31). This is precisely the construction we followed in each panel of Fig. 9 to define the GT T (from top to bottom) of the model M into a preferred basis. In panel b), the top model M has a single CC, which can be fully generated by the operator μ1 = (1 , 1) . We used this operator to create a preferred basis for M, by defining the first basis element of the GT T as σ1 = ϕμ1 (s) = s1s2 and choosing the second basis element to be independent from σ1. In panel a), the model M has two ICCs; we constructed a preferred basis by combining the elements of a basis ba for each ICC Ma, for which any choice of ra independent operators in Ma can be used. The operators μ1 = (1 , 1, 0) and μ2 = (1 , 0, 2) defining the first two variables of the preferred basis, σ1 = ϕμ1 (s) = s1s2 and σ2 = ϕμ2 (s) = s1s23 , are two independent operators of the rank-2

ICC of M. The operator μ3 = (0 , 0, 1) , which defines the third basis element σ3 = ϕμ3 (s) = s3, is an independent operator of the other ICC of M (which is already in a compact form). Upon the GT T , the model M is transformed into the bottom model M′, which appears in a compact form with the rank-2 ICC based solely on the first two variables. 

Property 35. Construction of a preferred basis of an MCM. A preferred basis b∗ of an MCM 

M = ∪a∈A Ma is constructed by combining the elements of a basis bMa for each ICC Ma:

b∗ = ∪a∈A bMa ∪ bM , (122) 

where bM is a set of (n − r) independent operators that complete the spin basis, with r = rank( M). For each component Ma, any set of ra = rank( Ma) independent operators in Ma can be chosen to define the basis 

bMa = ( ϕμa 

> 1

, · · · , ϕ μara ). The preferred basis of an MCM is therefore not necessarily unique. 

Using the construction above, any MCM can be represented in a preferred basis, in which the MCM corresponds to a partition of the basis variables, where each part of ra variables identifies an ICC of rank ra.This implies that MCMs with ICCs of the same ranks are related by a GT, because they can be mapped to the same partition in a preferred basis, and therefore they belong to the same equivalence class. This gives the following property, characterizing equivalence classes of MCMs: 

Property 36. Equivalence classes of MCMs. Consider an MCM with m ICCs and denote by mra the number of ICCs of rank ra in the model, such that m = ∑nra=1 mra . For a given value of q and number of spins n, all the MCMs with the same sequence of multiplicities {mra }1≤ra≤n of their ICC ranks are equivalent under gauge transformation, i.e. for any two such model, there exists a GT that can transform one into the other. They all belong to the same equivalence class of MCMs, which is identified by the sequence of multiplicities {mra }1≤ra≤n.Proof. We give a sketch of the proof. Consider two MCMs Mx and My that have the same sequence of multiplicities {mra }1≤ra≤n (i.e., the same rank list {ra}a∈A ). We specify two GTs, Tx for Mx and Ty for 

My , that bring each model into a preferred basis (in which each MCM corresponds to a partition of the basis elements). We design the two GTs such that they map ICCs of the same rank in each model to the same basis elements (for example, an ICC of rank 2 could be mapped to the variables σ3 and σ4 for both MCMs). This is possible because the two models have ICCs of the same ranks. As a result, the transformed models 

Tx[Mx] and Ty [My ] are identical MCMs (they correspond to the same partition of the basis elements), which 54 arXiv Template A Preprint 

means that Mx and My are equivalent. Composing the GTs, we can also write that My = T −1 

> y

[Tx[Mx]] ,where T −1 

> y

◦ T x is a GT. As a consequence, the number of different classes of MCMs with the same rank r is obtained by enumer-ating all possible (unordered) rank list {ra}a satisfying the constraint that ∑ 

> a∈A

ra = r. This corresponds to all possible integer partitions of r.

Property 37. Number of classes of MCMs of rank r. In a q-state spin system with n spins, the number of equivalence classes of MCMs of rank r (where r ≤ n) is given by the number of integer partitions of r, independently of the value of q.

This number is the same as in the binary case [23], because MCMs of different classes only differ by the rank of their ICCs, independently of the value of q (see Fig. 8 and 11). For example, for any integer value of q,there are two equivalence classes of MCMs with rank r = 2 (the class of MCMs with two ICCs of rank 1

each, and the class of MCMs with a single ICC of rank 2), three equivalence classes for r = 3 , and five for 

r = 4 (see complexity landscape in Fig. 11). The value of q instead changes the number of MCMs in each equivalence class. 

Property 38. Number of MCMs in a class. For a q-state spin system with n spins, the number of models in the class of MCMs defined by the sequence of multiplicities {mra }1≤ra≤n (where mra denotes the number of ICCs of rank ra in the models of the class) is given by (see App. D.3): 

NMCM (n, {mra }1≤ra≤n) = Nq (n, r )

The result for q prime was obtained using the same reasoning as in the binary case [23]. 

For example, for n = 3 and q = 3 , there are 117 MCMs with two ICCs of respective rank r1 = 1 and r2 = 2 ;Fig. 9.a shows two of them. The total number of spin models that are MCMs in an n-spin system can be obtained by summing the value in Eq. (123) over all possible integer partitions of r, for all r from 1 to n.The invariance of the family of MCMs under gauge transformations allows one to approach model selection from a representation invariant perspective, and ultimately design model selection procedures able to capture patterns of the data (such as symmetries, co-dependencies) in a way that is independent of the basis in which the data is initially observed. This is not possible for instance if one restricts the selection to pairwise models, because the family of pairwise spin models is not invariant under gauge transformation. Figure 6.a gives an example of a GT that transforms a pairwise model into a model with operators of higher order (in this case, there is a 3-spin operator). 

5.3 Statistical properties of MCMs 

Just like in the binary case [23], when represented in a preferred basis, an MCM corresponds to a partition of a subset of the basis variables in which each part identifies an ICC: the variables are fully connected inside the parts, with all operators of all orders, and not connected between the parts (see the models in Fig. 9 bottom). The MCM thus completely models all the statistical dependencies within each part (each ICC), but no statistical dependencies at all between the parts, as if they were independent (see Fig. 8, in the right panel, subspaces of different colors are considered statistically independent by the MCM in the left 55 arXiv Template A Preprint 

panel). This peculiar structure of MCMs endows them with properties that make modeling data with MCMs relatively efficient, in comparison to other common modeling approaches. In particular, the independence between the ICCs allows writing the model probability distribution as a product of marginal distributions over each ICC, when expressed in a preferred basis (see Fig. 8 and 10). Moreover, the completeness of the ICCs enables a re-parametrization of the components, with which most of the quantities of interest in the context of statistical inference can be easily computed (see next section 5.4). Below, we formalize these two properties. When written in a preferred basis, there are no dependencies between the basis variables corresponding to different ICCs of an MCM. This means that the multivariate probability distribution of the MCM factorizes over the basis elements of its ICCs. We recall that a preferred basis b∗ is a spin basis for the n spin system, which means that it can be used to define a GT T of the form s′ = b∗(s) (see definition 17). 

Property 39. Factorization of MCMs over their ICCs. The factorization and completeness properties of MCMs mean that, at best fit, an MCM corresponds to an approximation of the data that neglects all statistical dependencies between the variables of different ICCs and preserves all the other statistical patterns. In the context of model selection, the best MCM for a given dataset therefore corresponds to a factorization of the model probability distribution that best matches the structure of the data, optimally balancing goodness-of-fit of the data against model complexity (methods for this selection are discussed in the next section). This optimal factorization could be in the original basis of the data or in any other gauge-transformed basis (see Fig. 10). At best fit, this MCM thus provides a factorization of the empirical distribution that optimally approximates the data, by neglecting statistical dependencies between variables where they are the least relevant (variables assigned to different ICCs) and preserving them where they are the most relevant (within ICCs). In a given basis, the number of possible factorizations over r basis elements corresponds to the number of partitions of these r variables into distinct parts, which is equal to the Bell number of r, denoted Br . The number of different MCMs that factorize in the same preferred basis (denoted M CM ∗) is therefore obtained by summing this number over all possible model ranks, from r = 0 to n, and accounting for the possible choices of the r basis operators on which the model is based: 

NM CM ∗ (n) = 

> n

∑

> r=0

(nr

)

Br . (128) This number doesn’t depend on the value of q and is thus identical to the binary case (see Fig. 8). 

5.4 Statistical inference and model selection with MCMs 

A significant advantage of Minimally Complex Models over other parametric models for discrete data is that most useful quantities for statistical modeling have a closed-form analytical expression and are thus cheap to compute numerically. Similarly to the binary case [23], the factorization property 39 of the model probability distribution implies that the likelihood and the evidence (7) of an MCM also factorize over the ICCs when written in a preferred basis, and the equivalent parametrization property 40 makes these quantities easy to compute analytically for each ICC. Below, we give closed-form expressions for the maximum likelihood, the geometric complexity appearing in the MDL principle Eq. (8), and the model evidence (7). These results (see Appendix D for proofs) are direct extensions of the formulae obtained in the binary case [23] to the more general discrete case. Consider a q-state spin system with n spins represented by the discrete variables α = ( α1, · · · , α n) ∈

(Z/q Z)n and a dataset composed of N observations of the system denoted ˆα = ( α(1) , · · · , α(N )). We assume that the system is stationary and that the observed states are randomly sampled from the same underlying distribution, p(α | g, M), defined by a parametric model M. Under this assumption, the probability that the model M with parameters g assigns to the data, namely the likelihood of the model, is P ( ˆα | g, M) = 

∏Ni=1 p(α(i) | g, M). In the following, we take the model M to be an MCM M = ∪a∈A Ma. We consider a preferred basis of M of the form B∗ = ∪a∈A Ba ∪ BM, where each Ba is a basis of the ICC Ma, and denote by M′ = ∪a∈A M′ 

> a

the representation of M in this basis (i.e., M′ = TB∗ [M] and M′ 

> a

= TB∗ [Ma]

for all a ∈ A ). Figure 10: (colors online) Representation of the model discrete probability distribution in state space for two ICCs of Fig. 9. Each dot represents a state of the spin system and the color represents the probability of the state. States with the same color have the same probability. Top panel. Representation of the probability distribution of the MCM with a single ICC in Fig. 9.b. In the best basis, defined as the color variables (α′

1, α ′

2) in the bottom panel of Fig. 9.b, one can see directly that the model probability distribution factorizes and is uniform along the direction of α′

2, which gives the state space representation in the top left panel. After the GT (α1, α 2) = T −1[( α′

1, α ′

2)] (top MCM in Fig. 9.b), the probability distribution remains uniform in the direction of α′

2, which is perpendicular to the direction of α′

1 = α1 + α2. This is a non-trivial factorization when looking at the system of variables (α1, α 2). Bottom panel. Representation of the probability distribution of the single ICC based on the spin variables (σ1, σ 2) in the bottom panel of Fig. 9.a. We denote by (α′

1, α ′

.A useful quantity for model selection is the model evidence (or marginal likelihood) defined in Eq. (7). Selecting the model with the largest evidence, instead of the largest maximum likelihood, allows basing the selection of the model not only on the goodness of its fit to the data, but also on its complexity. Within a family of models, the model with the largest evidence achieves an optimal balance between goodness-of-fit and simplicity. Here we compute the evidence assuming Jeffreys’ prior [47] over the parameters (see App. D.9). Using the factorization property 39 of MCM, Jeffreys’ prior and the model evidence factorize over the ICCs, and, using the completeness of the ICCs, we obtain a closed-form expression for the model evidence (see App. D.10): 59 arXiv Template A Preprint

The evidence of an ICC in Eq. (136) only depends on its rank ra, on the empirical distribution of the reduced dataset ˆα′ 

> a

= Ba( ˆα), and on the number N of datapoints. The evidence of a statistical model is usually expensive to compute from Eq. (7), due to the high-dimensional integral. Thanks to the closed-form expression (136), the evidence of any MCM can be computed efficiently with minimal computational resources, making it feasible in practice to perform model selection among MCMs on systems of reasonably large sizes (with hundreds of variables). In Sec. 6, we apply this approach to real data. Models can also be selected following the minimum description length (MDL) principle [18–20], for which the best model is the one providing the most compressed representation of the data. Equation (8) gives the description length of a spin model for a sufficiently large number N of samples (expanded to order O(1) 

in N ). The last term cM in the expansion is the geometric complexity of the model. It is defined as a high-dimensional integral (A80), which can be computed analytically for MCMs (see App. D.7). 

Property 44. Geometric complexity of MCMs. Thanks to the independence between ICCs, the geo-metric complexity of an MCM is given by the sum of the complexity of its ICCs:
