Figure 11 shows the complexity landscape of MCMs for different values of q. Observe a shift of the entire landscape towards larger complexities for increasing values of q, reflecting the increase in the number of parameters with q. Note the two limit lines, sub-complete models (MCMs with a single ICC) are the simplest among all MCMs with the same number of parameters and rank, while IMCMs are the most complex. For binary systems, Ref. [23] has hypothesized that classes of MCMs are the classes of lowest complexity among models with the same number of parameters and the same rank (hence their name). Although they verified that this is the case for systems with n = 4 spins or less, this hypothesis has not been proved in the more general case. However, it is interesting to point out that MCMs likely span a large part of the complexity landscape of all q-state spin models, because they cover a large set of combinations of parameter number and rank. Indeed, at fixed number of parameters, it is expected that the higher the rank of the model (number of degrees of freedom of the model), the higher its complexity [15]. In the binary case, Ref. [15] showed that the sub-complete models are the simplest among all models with the same number of parameters and rank, and the independent models (which are MCMs when q = 2 ) are the most complex. For a large number N of samples, the expansion of the negative log-evidence assuming Jeffreys’ prior matches the expansion of the description length L( ˆα | M ) in Eq. (8) [21, 22]. In App. D.11, we verify that 60 arXiv Template A Preprint 

Figure 11: (colors online) Complexity cM of all MCMs with n = 4 − 5 spins for varying values of 

q. For a given value of q, all the models with the same rank r and rank sequence {ra}a∈A of their ICCs are equivalent. As a consequence, the number of classes of MCMs in an n-spin system is the same for all values of q. Here, one observes the shift in complexity of these classes as one increases the value of q. The two dashed lines indicate the complexity of IMCMs (red) and the complexity of sub-complete models (black). this is indeed the case for the closed-form expression (136) of the log-evidence of MCMs and give the next order term of the expansion. The advantage of using the closed-form expression (136) of the model evidence over the description length expansion (8) is that it is exact for any value of N and it is cheap to compute in practice. In particular, it provides an accurate measure for comparing models when the number of datapoints is small or, more precisely, in undersampling cases. 

5.5 Measuring data complexity with MCMs 

The minimum description length (MDL) principle was introduced by Rissanen in the context of measuring the complexity of a noisy dataset. Without compression, a dataset with n q -state discrete variables and 

N datapoints requires N × n qits to be encoded, i.e., one qit per variable per datapoint, where one qit corresponds to a unit of information of (log q) nats (we use the term “qit” for this unit by analogy to bit and trit). Using a statistical model, redundant patterns in the data can be encoded more concisely (encoding the patterns one time instead of multiple times), which yields a compression of the data. The resulting number of qits required to encode a dataset ˆα using a statistical model M is given by the description length L( ˆα | M )

(taking the logarithm in base q). The minimum description length achieved (i.e., maximal compression) within a family of statistical models is interpreted by Rissanen [18] as a measure of stochastic complexity of the data, by analogy with algorithmic definitions of complexity (such as Kolmogorov complexity). Extending the parallel between the description length and the negative log-evidence to finite values of N , we propose to use the negative log-evidence of the best MCM as a measure of stochastic complexity of the data within the family of MCMs. In Sec. 6, we give an example of the application of this quantity as a measure of data complexity. 

Definition 34. Complexity of the data. We propose to use the negative log-evidence of the best MCM (which is the MCM with the largest evidence) as a measure of stochastic complexity of the data within the family of MCMs: 

COMP M CM ( ˆα) = − max  

> M∈ MCM

log P ( ˆα | M ) , (139) 

where the evidence P ( ˆα | M ) of an MCM is given in Property 43. 

6 Applications to real data 

6.1 Search algorithms: optimization of the log-Evidence of MCMs 

Despite the advantage provided by the closed-form expression for the evidence in Eq. (136), the number of MCMs is still large and in practice searching exhaustively for the best MCM among all remains com-putationally untractable for systems with more than ten variables. Fortunately however, finding the best MCM (or near-optimal MCM) among those sharing a common preferred basis is computationally accessible, thanks to a direct mapping between these models and partitions of the basis variables. This one-to-one correspondence with partitions allows for the development of efficient search algorithms beyond exhaustive enumeration. In a given preferred basis, finding the best MCM corresponds to finding the factorization of the model probability distribution over the basis variables that best matches the structure of the data. Ref. [39] likens finding such MCM factorization in the original basis of the data to a high-order community detection, and thus is interesting on its own. To simplify the model selection procedure in the binary case, Ref. [23] also hypothesized that the best MCM overall would be obtained in the basis formed by the best independent model (i.e., the one with the largest evidence) and thus proposed to divide the selection procedure in two steps: first find the best independent model and use it as a new basis for the data, and then find an optimal 61 arXiv Template A Preprint 

factorization in this basis. We follow a similar approach for the discrete case, focusing on two separate steps: searching for an optimal spin basis and searching for the best MCM in a chosen preferred basis. 

6.1.1 Search for the optimal MCM in a given preferred basis 

In a given preferred basis, each MCM maps to a partition of the spin variables into independent parts, independently of the value of q. This makes it possible to use the same search algorithms as in the binary case, with the only difference being that the model evidence is now computed using the more general formula (136). Following Ref. [23], we used two different algorithms: 1) an exhaustive search, going through all possible partitions of the n variables using a Gray code [48, 49], which can be used for small systems with up to about 15 variables; and 2) a greedy search, that starts from the independent model and successively merges the two ICCs that lead to the largest increase in evidence until the evidence reaches a maximum, which can be used for larger systems (our current implementation [50] can be used with up to 128 variables). Ref [50] also implements two other search algorithms not used in this paper, a simulated annealing search and a greedy search using divisive hierarchical clustering. 

6.1.2 Search for an optimal basis 

In the binary case, Ref. [23] proposes to use the best independent model with n operators as a preferred basis for the MCM search. The n independent operators of this model correspond to the spin basis in which the empirical probability distribution is the most likely to factorize over each variable as if there were independent. We extend this idea to the more general discrete case, by searching for a spin basis in which the empirical probability distribution is most likely to factorize. In other words, we search for the best IMCM for the data and any basis for that IMCM equally satisfies the requirement above. Using any of these bases as the preferred basis for the MCM search (in the previous section) will give the same set of MCMs to choose from (because ICCs always include all possible multiples of the basis operators). In the binary case, an IMCM is (also) an independent model, and we recover the approach proposed in [23]. The best IMCM with n ICCs (each of rank 1) is defined as the one with the largest evidence. Because all IMCMs with n ICCs are in the same equivalence class, the model with the largest evidence among them is also the one that has the largest maximum likelihood. Using Eq. (133), the maximum log-likelihood of an IMCM with n ICCs based on the spin basis B = ( μ1, . . . , μn) (where each Ba = ( μa) is a basis of one of the ICC) is given by: 

log P ( ˆα | ˆg, Mimcm ) = −N

Equation (140) therefore implies that the most likely IMCM is given by the set of the n least entropic independent operators in the data 33 .Exactly as in the binary case, finding the most likely IMCM corresponds to finding the set of n independent operators with the lowest entropy. The only difference is that now there are multiple bases of operators that correspond to the same IMCM and thus work equivalently as a preferred basis (they identify the same set of MCMs on that basis).       

> 33 Note that for q > 2, finding the most likely IMCM is different from finding the most likely independent model. An independent model with noperators can be written as a set of nindependent components with one operator in each component, but these components are not complete. This means that, for an independent model, the Kullback-Leibler divergence between the single operator and the data within each independent component is not zero (contrary to IMCMs). This term would then have to be taken into account in the computation of the maximum likelihood of the independent model.

62 arXiv Template A Preprint 

To find these operators for small systems (with n ≲ 15 ), we extend the exhaustive algorithm of Ref. [23]. We compute the entropy of all the operators of the system (excluding certain operators, listed below) and create an (n × K)-matrix containing in columns the discrete representations μj of the K least entropic operators, ordered from low to high entropy. The elements of this matrix are in (Z/q Z). Identifying the n

least entropic independent operators then consists of finding the independent columns in this matrix starting from the left-hand side. This can be done using Gaussian elimination in (Z/q Z): the columns with leading coefficients identify the set of least entropic independent operators. For q non-prime, some interactions are “not invertible” and are directly excluded from this procedure; these are the interactions μ for which there exists a coefficient c ∈ (Z/q Z) such that cμ = 0 in (Z/q Z)n (see Sec. 4.1). Moreover for q > 2, all the considered (invertible) operators μ have a non-identical complex conjugate −μ; conjugate operators have the same empirical entropy and for the preferred basis it doesn’t matter if one uses μ or −μ as a basis element because they identify the same preferred basis 34 (any partition of the basis elements into ICCs will give the same MCM independently of using μ or −μ as a basis element). Therefore, only one element from each conjugate pair is included in the procedure (in our implementation [50], this is done by keeping only the interaction vectors μ whose first entry is smaller or equal to q/ 2). Similarly, we can also exclude from the selection all multiples of the already selected operators, because they identify different bases of the same IMCMs 35 . This wasn’t implemented, but would improve further the speed of the code. With this exhaustive approach, the number of operators to consider grows exponentially as qn, making this algorithm unusable in practice for systems of more than 25 variables. For many real systems, it seems reasonable to assume that the least entropic operators will be of low order (i.e., of order 1, 2, 3, or 4), and one could reduce the exhaustive search to consider operators up to a fixed order k only. In the binary case, Ref. [23] also proposes an iterative algorithm that can consider operators of any order while keeping the computational time similar to fixing k. This algorithm can be easily extended to larger values of q; it is available in [50] but not used in this paper. 

6.2 Code availability and reproducibility 

The codes for all three algorithms can be found in our Python package in Ref. [50] (C++ codes with Python bindings). A notable difference with respect to the codes for binary MCMs provided by Ref. [23] is that datapoints and operators cannot be easily encoded by binary integers anymore. This is because the state vectors α and the interaction vectors μ are not necessarily binary, but more generally defined in (Z/q Z)n. To avoid losing efficiency, we encoded these vectors using several 128-bit integers to encode 128 q-state variables (i.e., each q-state variable is encoded by log 2(q) bits). This is to compensate for the lack of built-in data structure to encode integers in base q in our chosen language (C++) but also in most common programming languages. As a proof of concept, we revisit two datasets previously analyzed with MCMs in the binary setting [23]. We study the impact of the choices made for how to discretize the data and of the resolution q of discretization on the structure of the resulting MCM. 

6.3 Example 1: binary dataset with artificially increased values of q, embedded in a larger space 

For illustration purposes, we first analyze a binary dataset using MCMs with varying values of q, as if the (binary) variables were taking values in [0 , q − 1] . The dataset comes from the US Supreme Court database [51] and consists of the votes of the n = 9 justices on N = 895 cases debated during the second Rehnquist Court. For q = 2 , voting outcomes were labeled as in Ref. [52] using the political inclination (if known) of each debated case, such that a judge casts a vote αj = 1 if the decision is conservative-oriented, or αj = 0 if it is liberal-oriented. Although the datapoints are binary, one can embed them into the larger

The grey circles indicate the partitions corresponding to the best MCMs for smaller values of q. The log-evidence of MCMs corresponding to the same partition decreases with q, due to the increase in complexity penalty. In particular, no partition lies above the (x = y)-line (indicated by the shaded area) and the vertical distance between this line and a dot corresponds to the increase in penalty in the log-evidence when changing q

from 2 to, respectively, 3, 4, or 5 for the corresponding partition. MCMs with ICCs of the same sizes are penalized by the same amount and corresponds to aligned dots parallel to the (x = y)-line. For the MCMs with nICCs = 2 (orange dots), we indicated the size of the ICCs near the alignment: for example “ [4 , 5] ”denotes MCMs with one ICC of size 4 and one of size 5. Models with fewer ICCs have a larger average ICC size and thus get a stronger penalty with increasing q (see orange dots moving faster down than other dots from one panel to the next). 64 arXiv Template A Preprint 

state space (Z/q Z)n, effectively increasing the number of possible configurations while keeping the observed states unchanged. Embedding the data into a larger space is common in data analysis. For instance, when using principal component analysis on discrete data, the data is analyzed as if the variables were taking real values even though they are discrete. 

6.3.1 Embedding choice 

For q > 2, to reflect the opposite political valence between conservative and liberal oriented votes, we gave opposite values of αj to opposite votes, assigning αj = 1 to liberal-oriented votes and αj = −1 (mod q) = (q − 1) to conservative-oriented votes. This choice confers conjugate spin values sj = exp(2 iπα j /q ) to these two states. All the other states of αj are not observed in the data. The specific choice of mapping of αj

from binary to discrete values will not impact the outcome of the best MCM in the original basis of the data (compared to any other permutations of the values of αj ). However, it can impact the interactions selected in the best basis (and the interactions of the best spin model overall) and change the interpretation of what a given interaction means (see Sec. 2.4 on interpreting the interactions). Intuitively, we expect that labeling the discrete data in a way that best reflects the meaning associated with the values taken by the variables in the studied system would make it easier to interpret the uncovered interactions. On the other hand, assigning the values at random can make it more difficult to interpret the model. Overall, the final result from the modeling procedure (i.e., the interpreted model) should be consistent across mapping choices. In other words, the uncovered models for different choices should be related mathematically by the (bijective) transformation of variables associated with the different mapping choices). In the following, we first analyze the impact of the embedding on the optimal MCM factorization in the original basis of the data, and then on the overall best MCM. 

6.3.2 Optimal MCM factorization in the original basis 

Figure 12 Top shows the best MCMs found by the exhaustive search in the original basis of the data for varying values of q. For q = 3 , we found the same factorization as in the binary case [23], with the five conservative-oriented justices grouped in one ICC and the four more liberal-oriented justices grouped in a second ICC. Further increasing q breaks these components into smaller ICCs, first splitting the conservative group at q = 4 and then the liberal group at q = 5 . The reduction of the ICC sizes with larger q can be understood intuitively. For larger values of q, ICCs based on the same number ra of variables require more parameters (precisely, qra − 1 parameters) and are thus more penalized. Because the observed states remain unchanged, we therefore expect the ICCs of the best MCM to get smaller as we artificially increase q.Formally, consider a dataset with variables taking values α ∈ (Z/q 1Z)n, and consider two MCMs, Mq1

and Mq2 , corresponding to the same partition of the n basis variables but for two different values of q,respectively q1 and q2 with q2 > q 1. For these models, the log-evidence takes the form (using r = n in Eq. (135) and (136)): 

log P ( ˆα | M ) = ∑

)

 . (141) Here, A denotes the common partition identifying both Mq1 and Mq2 , such that each element a ∈ A 

identifies an ICC in each model based on the same groups of variables αa. Because the dataset itself remains unchanged (with states α ∈ (Z/q 1Z)n) and because the two MCMs correspond to the same partition of the basis variables, the second sum in Eq. (141) takes the same value for both models. Only the complexity part (first term within the first sum) changes with q, becoming more negative for larger q at fixed value of ra.Figure 12 Bottom illustrates how this increase in penalty with q impacts different models. MCMs with ICCs of the same sizes {ra} (which belong to the same complexity class) are penalized by the same amount as one increases q (see the alignment of the dots parallel to the (x = y)-line) and the penalty increases faster with 

q for MCMs with larger average ICC size (i.e. with a smaller number of ICCs). These effects can also be deduced from the dependence in {ra} of the complexity term in Eq. (141). As a result, the best MCM can 65 arXiv Template A Preprint 

structurally change when increasing q, favoring models with smaller ICCs (see changing location of the red circle towards MCMs with smaller ICCs). 

6.3.3 Best MCM factorization overall 

Figure 13 shows the best MCM found overall for different values of q, by performing first an exhaustive search for the optimal basis, and then an exhaustive search for the optimal MCM on that basis. The nine independent operators of the best basis are represented by squares in the figure. Except for one single spin operator on AS, all these operators are pairwise and identify interactions between the same pairs of justices for all values of q. In the binary case, [23] reported that these 9 interactions alone account for 86% of the information extracted by a fully connected pairwise model with 45 interactions. A notable difference with the binary case, is that these pairwise operators are of the form μij = (1 , q − 1) between two justices i and j, and not of the form μij = (1 , 1) as one could have naively extrapolated from the binary case (where μij denotes the interaction vector μ reduced to the i-th and j-th entries). This is because the most frequent pattern between these pairs of justices is the symmetric voting pattern αi = αj , which is a pattern that is best captured by this type of pairwise interaction (see the explanations in Example 7 of Sec. 2.4.2). Indeed, the justices SO and SB, whose interaction is the most entropic pairwise interaction in the basis, vote identically in 78% of the cases, and AS and CT, whose pairwise interaction is the least entropic interaction among all, vote identically in 93% of the cases. Note that the choice of embedding doesn’t impact the best basis in this case, because the symmetric patterns αi = αj identified by the best basis remain unchanged under permutation of the state values. In Fig. 13, the basis operators are numbered from the least to the most entropic. Observe that this ordering changes slightly from q = 2 to q = 3 (see the operators ranked 7 and 8, and 2 and 3). This is because for q = 2 the entropy of an operator only depends on the probability of the operator to be even (symmetric state αi = αj ) or odd (antisymmetric states αi = −αj ), while for q ≥ 3

the entropy of a pairwise operator also depends on the relative occurrence of the non-symmetric states. The ICCs of the best MCMs found on the optimal basis for each q are identified by the triangles in Fig. 13. These MCMs have ICCs of smaller sizes (on average) than those found in the original basis for the same value of q (shown in Fig. 12), which means that they correspond to less complex models (with fewer interactions). The log-evidence of these models (see table 2) is also larger than that of the MCMs based on the original basis. This means that they are better models for the data, which is what we expected from the two-step procedure. Similarly to the analysis in the original basis, ICCs are smaller for larger q, which can be explained by the increase in penalty with q while the data patterns remain mostly unchanged. ICCs for larger q also seem to lie mostly within the ICCs for smaller q, although this doesn’t hold for all ICCs. 6.4 Example 2: Choice of discretization and standardization of discrete data. 

In this second example, we analyze answers to the Big Five Personality Test [53]. In particular, we discuss possible choices of discretization schemes and their impact on the best MCM factorization in the original basis. The test consists of n = 50 questions designed to probe the personality of the respondents along five dimensions generally considered as describing the main traits of personality: extraversion, emotional stability, agreeableness, conscientiousness, and openness to experience. Each trait is measured based on the answers to ten statements on a scale of x = 1 (disagree) to 5 (agree). For example, extraversion is probed by statements such as “I am the life of the party” or “I am quiet around strangers” . We obtained a dataset from the Open-Source Psychometrics Project [54] with answers from N = 602 591 individuals (after cleaning for errors and identical IP addresses). The dataset was then converted from the original 1-to-5 scale to the desired discrete formats, with respectively q = 2 , 3, or 5, using two different discretization schemes. In the following, we only perform the MCM analysis in the original basis of the data. This is because we are mainly interested in studying the impact of different choices of discretization of the data and the original variables were purposely chosen in the design of the Big Five test. As a side note, in the binarized version, [23] found that the best MCM in the best basis happened to be is identical to the best MCM found in the original basis. 

6.4.1 Discretization Scheme 1: direct mapping 

For the first scheme, we re-assign the values of the variables xj ∈ { 1, 2, 3, 4, 5} in the original data (where xj

denotes the answer to the j-th question) to new discrete values αj ∈ (Z/q Z) for q = 2 , 3 and 5, as described in Table 1 Scheme 1. To respect the opposite valence between positive and negative answers, we associated the “neutral” answer to αj = 0 and gave opposite values 36 to opposite answers (e.g., αj = 1 to “agree” and 

αj = −1 (mod q) = ( q − 1) to “disagree”). Choosing this assignment of αj doesn’t affect the outcome of the best MCM with respect to selecting any other permutations of the values of αj (because permuting the values of αj doesn’t change the numbers kαa of occurrence of the states used in Eq. (141)). However, as mentioned previously, if the final goal is to obtain a more precise high-order spin model for the data (e.g., beyond MCMs), then different choices can lead to different optimal models, and a choice informed by domain knowledge can make it easier to interpret the uncovered model. Figure 14 shows the best MCMs found in the original basis of the data for Scheme 1. From q = 2 to 

q = 3 , the sizes of the ICCs remain large with 9 to 10 variables: the dataset is sufficiently rich at q = 3 

for the balance between fitting and complexity to require ICCs of a similar sizes than at q = 2 (contrary to Example 1). In other words, the increase in size of the state space is compensated by an increase in richness of the data. From q = 3 to q = 5 , the ICCs break down into smaller components. Although questions are mostly grouped into ICCs corresponding to the same traits, we also find mismatches (especially for larger q). Additionally, we observe that some statements are more biased than others towards agreeing or disagreeing (e.g., “I insult others”), which may give rise to spurious correlations. The next scheme will attempt to reduce this effect by standardizing the data. 

6.4.2 Discretization Scheme 2: standardization 

In the second scheme, the value of each variable xj was re-centered around its empirical mean ¯xj following Scheme 2 in Table 1. Besides, for q = 3 and 5, we defined small intervals on the values of xj of size 2σj ϵ

within which αj takes a constant value, where σj denotes the standard deviation of xj . The value of ϵ was then adjusted to maximize the entropy of the variables in the data (see Fig. A2 in Appendix). Here as well, we reflected the opposite valence of the data between positive and negative answers by giving opposite values of αj to opposite intervals in xj . The introduction of Scheme 2 is motivated by the observation that some statements are significantly more biased than others. For example, the answer to the statement “ I insult others ” is more often negative, with 62% of the respondents who disagree and only 21% who agree ( ¯xj ≃ 2.27 ). However, such patterns are inherent to the choice of the question, and are not directly informative about    

> 36 corresponding to conjugate values of sj= exp(2 iπα j/q )

67 arXiv Template A Preprint 

personality. Instead, one may understand personality from the way respondents answer with respect to the average behavior in the population. In this context, it would be more interesting to model patterns between answers based on their deviation from the population average instead of raw dependencies. We achieved this in Scheme 2 by standardizing each variable and maximizing their entropy before modeling them, so that individual variables carry as little information as possible. For example, staying “neutral” for the statement “ I insult others ” ( 17% of the respondents) tends to be more negative than the majority of people, and recentering the data will then move “neutral” answers to the negative side. Similarly, the statement “ I spend time reflecting on things ” has the lowest entropy (see Fig. A2 in Appendix), with 48% 

of the respondents “strongly agreeing“ and 34% “agreeing“ ( ¯xj ≃ 4.22 ); recentering the data will then give “neutral“ answers to respondents “agreeing“ in their response. Figure 14 shows the best MCMs found in the original basis of the data for Scheme 2. They reveal a clearer separation of the five traits than in Scheme 1, in particular for q = 3 and 5. In this example, reducing the bias of the variables allows us to better focus the model on the (high-order) correlation structure of the data, following a principle similar to using z-scored variables in Principal Component Analysis and revealing in this case a clearer separation between the traits. We note that the standardization of the data is not a requirement of the technique, but is a specific choice that can be made depending on what type of dependencies one is interested in modeling (i.e., row dependencies or correlations). 7 Discussion 

In this work, we introduced a complete family of maximum entropy models designed to model any high-order patterns of discrete data. This comprehensive framework is capable of capturing the full complexity of multivariate discrete systems. In contrast to the version of the Potts models typically used in statistical inference (see Eq. (29)), our models avoid over-parametrization and are able to capture high-order patterns. Focusing strictly on the pairwise case, for example, the maximum number of parameters used corresponds exactly to the number of pairwise patterns that can be fitted to the data. Furthermore, mapping these maximum entropy models to discrete Fourier analysis not only confirms that they represent a complete model family for discrete data, but also allows us to straightforwardly invert the parameters from empirical frequencies via a discrete Fourier transform. A key analytical result of our framework is the cluster expansion of the partition function of q-state spin models, which we express in terms of the loops of the models. In practice, these loops can be extracted easily using the matrix representation of spin models. Furthermore, the cluster expansion of the partition function establishes a direct connection between the statistical properties of the models and the algebraic properties of their matrix representation. This transition to a linear algebraic perspective facilitates the manipulation of spin models and allows for a rigorous characterization of statistical constraints, model complexity, and equivalence classes. In particular, we extended the framework of equivalence classes of spin models by generalizing the notion of gauge transformations (GTs) to the discrete setting. We showed that GTs provide a more general definition of symmetries in discrete data, extending well beyond standard spatial reflections, rotations, or permutations. More importantly, GTs enable the development of model selection 70 arXiv Template A Preprint 

procedures that are entirely independent of the original basis representation of the data. This opens the way for equivariant modeling, allowing systems to be analyzed based on their inherent symmetries rather than arbitrary representational choices. While the family of q-state spin models has a super-exponential number of models, we make the frame-work more widely usable in practice by focusing on a sub-family of models, the minimally complex models (MCMs). MCMs operate essentially as factorization models: they allow us to find a factorization of the multivariate probability distribution that optimally matches the underlying structure of the data by care-fully balancing goodness-of-fit against complexity. The benefit of working with MCMs is that all quantities of interest have a closed-form expression allowing for efficient model selection in reasonably large systems. Moreover, the family of MCMs is invariant under GTs enabling equivariant model selection within this family. We demonstrated the efficacy of this approach through real-world applications. In particular, we discussed the importance of the choice of the discretization scheme and illustrated how they affect the resulting models in our examples. To ensure broad accessibility, we have also released an open-source package [50] capable of analyzing systems with up to 128 discrete variables. Looking forward, interesting research directions are the development of more efficient search algorithms within the space of MCMs for scaling the analysis to larger systems, as well as extending our general modeling framework to continuous and non-stationary data. In particular, extending the MCM approach will broaden the scope of optimal factorization techniques to an even wider array of complex systems. Another interesting idea is the use of generalized symmetries of discrete data and spin models (thanks to the definition of gauge transformations) for the development of equivariant model selection. 71 arXiv Template A Preprint

A.5 Entropy of models with a single interaction 

Take a q-state spin model for n variables with a single interaction μ encoded by the operator ϕμ (and its c.c. if μ̸ = −μ). For all states α, the operator ϕμ(α) = zμ·α 

> q

only takes values in the q-th roots of unity (corresponding to q locations on the unit circle). Besides, if q is prime or if for all integer c ∈ [0 , q − 1] the vector μ verifies that cμ̸ = 0 in (Z/q Z)n, then there are the same number of states at each root (i.e., qn−1

states). For a given j ∈ (Z/q Z), all the states α for which μ · α = j have the same interaction energy (51),

B.2 Euler formula, special case μ ∈ M 0

Proof of Eq. (65) . From the definition (60), all the elements μ ∈ M 0 verify that 2 μ = 0 in (Z/q Z)n and, as a consequence, that [ϕμ(α)] 2 = 1 for all state α ∈ (Z/q Z)n. This means that for μ ∈ M 0 one can write 

exp( gμϕμ(α)) as a sum over two terms only after grouping, respectively, odd and even powers of ϕμ in the generalized Euler formula (A25). One then simply recovers the original Euler formula (A22): 

∀μ ∈ M 0, egμϕμ(α) = A0(gμ) + A1(gμ) ϕμ(α) , (A31)

This result can also be obtained directly by observing that for all μ ∈ M 0, ϕμ(α) = ±1, which immediately gives Eq. (A22). It is however interesting to recover it from the general formula by grouping odd and even powers of ϕμ, because a similar reasoning can also be used for other prime factors of q when q is not prime (as in Sec. 3.3.3).

Appendix C Gauge transformations 

C.1 Counting gauge transformations

> 1

> 2

|GL (( Z/p ai 

> i

Z)n)| . (A52) It remains to determine the order of GL (( Z/p aZ)n) for a prime p and a ∈ N.78 arXiv Template A Preprint 

Consider the canonical ring homomorphism ρ : GL (( Z/p aZ)n) → GL (( Z/p Z)n) which reduces each matrix entry modulo p. This map is a surjective group homomorphism. By the first isomorphism theorem for groups, we have: 

|GL (( Z/p aZ)n)| = | ker( ρ)| · | GL (( Z/p Z)n)| . (A53) The kernel, ker( ρ), consists of matrices M ∈ GL (( Z/p aZ)n) such that M ≡ I (mod p), where I is the identity matrix. Any such matrix can be written in the form M = I + pA , where A is an n × n matrix with entries in Z/p a−1Z. There are (pa−1)n2

possible matrices for A. Therefore, the order of the kernel is: 

| ker( ρ)| = ( pa−1)n2

= pn2(a−1) . (A54) The remaining term, |GL n(Z/p Z)|, is the order of the general linear group over the finite field Fp. This is a standard result obtained by counting the ways to choose n linearly independent column vectors from Fnp .The formula is: 

|GL (Fnp )| = ( pn − 1)( pn − p) · · · (pn − pn−1) = 

> n−1

∏

> k=0

(pn − pk) . (A55) Combining these results, the order for the prime power case is: 

|GL n(Z/p aZ)| = pn2(a−1) 

> n−1

∏

> k=0

(pn − pk) . (A56) The total number of gauge transformations for an arbitrary q is found by substituting this into the product over the prime factors of q.

(A57) corresponds to the Hamiltonian of a spin model with operators Tb[ϕμ]( s′) = ϕμ′

(s′) where μ′ = T −1μ. The energy of a state s under a model M is thus mapped by a GT to the energy of a new state s′ = b(s) of a new spin model M′:

D.4 Notations for the proofs in the sections below 

Throughout the paper, we consider a system of n discrete random variables αi that can take integer values between 0 and (q − 1) with q ≥ 2. A state of the system is denoted α = ( α1, · · · , α n) ∈ (Z/q Z)n, and a dataset is composed of N observations of the system and is denoted ˆα = ( α(1) , · · · , α(N )). We assume the system to be stationary and that the observed states are randomly sampled from the same underlying distribution, p(α | g, M), defined by a parametric model M.In the following, we consider that M is an MCM with a single Independent Complete Component (ICC) 

Ma of rank ra (with ra ≤ n). For the sake of simplicity of the derivations, we consider that M is already written in a preferred basis and we reduce the system to the ra variables modeled by Ma. This corresponds to reducing the space to the basis variables αa on which the ICC Ma is based, and considering the model distribution pa(αa | ga, Ma), which is a distribution over ra variables only (instead of n). To simplify the notation, we then drop the index “ a” used to label the ICCs. We denote the model Micc and its rank r, and use the notation α = ( α1, · · · , α r ) ∈ (Z/q Z)r for the state of the system reduced to the r variables (strictly speaking, these variables can be any subset of the variables α introduced in the previous paragraph). 82 arXiv Template A Preprint 

D.5 Alternative parametrization of an Independent Complete Components (ICC). 

The ICC Micc is a complete model (see Sec. (2)) for the sub-system of r = rank( Micc ) variables. The model has (qr − 1) parameters g and can be (equivalently) defined as the following parametric family of probability distributions:

is defined by the normalization:

has its first entry equal to 1, and all the other entries are equal to 0. The vector μ = ( qr − 1) has all its r

entries equal to (q − 1) . Substituting the expression of η0 in Eq. (A70), we get:

D.6 Fisher Information Matrix (FIM) for an ICC. 

For an MCM (with multiple ICCs), the Fisher Information Matrix (FIM) in Eq. (112) is block diagonal, where each block corresponds to the FIM restricted to a single ICC. This is because the model probability distribution of an MCM factorizes over its ICCs, which also have non-overlapping sets of parameters. Con-sequently, the determinant of the FIM of an MCM is the product of the determinant of the FIM for each ICC, i.e., for an MCM M = ∪a∈A Ma with parameters g = ∪a∈A ga:

det I(g) = ∏

> a∈A

det Ia(ga) . (A73) Let us now compute the FIM Ia for a single ICC. For simplicity, we consider a system reduced to a single ICC with the notations specified in Sec. (A72), the Fisher Information Matrix (FIM) is the square matrix of order (qr − 1) with elements: 

Iμν (η) = − 〈∂ημ ∂ην log p(α | η, Micc )〉 , ∀ μ, ν ∈ [( Z/q Z)r ]∗ , (A74) where the average is taken over the model distribution P (α | M icc , η). Substituting Eq. (A72) and taking the two derivatives gives, ∀ μ, ν ∈ [( Z/q Z)r ]∗:

D.11 Expansion of the log-evidence for large N and MDL principle. 

Bayesian model selection and Minimum Description length principle are already known to be equivalent for large dataset, assuming Jeffreys’ prior over the parameters. For completeness, we re-derive this result in the particular case of MCM. Let us focus on the evidence of the complete model, as the extension to MCM is straightforward from that result. Taking the logarithm of the evidence in Eq. (A94) yields: 

log P ( ˆα | M icc ) = ∑

> α∈(Z/q Z)r

)

> 2

> 2

) , (A96) where the last term can be recognized as the geometrical complexity cicc (r) of an ICC of rank r in Eq. (A84). We assume that the dataset ˆα is sufficiently well-sampled, such that the empirical probability pα = kα( ˆα)/N 

is non zero for all state (each state is observed at least a few times). We then take the expansion of the two log-gamma function for large N . This gives the following expansion of the log-evidence to the order of 1/N :

log P ( ˆα | M icc ) = N ∑

> α∈(Z/q Z)r

, (A97) where K = qr − 1 is the number of parameters in the ICC. The first term in the expansion is the maximum log-likelihood of the ICC (see Property 42), in which pα = kα( ˆα)/N is the empirical probability of state α

in the dataset ˆα. The second term is the first complexity term in the MDL principle, penalizing models with a large number K of parameters. Comparing Eq. (A97) with Eq. (8), one can see that selecting the MCM with the largest log-evidence, log P ( ˆα | M ), is equivalent at large N to selecting the MCM with the smallest description length, L( ˆα | M ).

Figure A2: Entropy (in base q) of the 50 variables in the Big Five Personality Test data for the two discretization schemes described in Sec. 6.4 with q = 3 and q = 5 . The two left panels give the value of the entropy in base q (or “qits”) for each variable for each discretization choice. The color indicates the magnitude of the entropy, which can vary between 0 and 1 qit. The variables are displayed in rows from left to right in the same order as in the original dataset [54] and each row corresponds to one trait (“Ex” for Extraversion, “ES” for Emotional stability, “A” for Agreeableness, “C” for Conscientiousness, and “OE” for Openness to experience). For example, in the raw data (i.e., scheme 1 with q = 5 ), the previous to last variable is the least entropic and corresponds to the question “ I spend time reflecting on things ”. The right panel shows the average entropy (red curve) and minimal entropy (blue curve) obtained as one varies the value of ϵ in Scheme 2. For the analysis in Sec. 6.4, the value of epsilon was chosen to maximize the entropy of the least entropic variable (as indicated by the vertical dashed line).
